- [Awesome Connected Things Security](https://github.com/V33RU/awesome-connected-things-sec) — collection · firmware, protocol — A single-README list of security-research references for IoT, embedded, industrial and automotive systems, organized into sections on hardware attacks, wireless protocols (BLE, Zigbee/Z-Wave, LoRa, Matter/Thread, cellular, NFC/RFID, Wi-Fi, USB), firmware security including extraction, static and dynamic analysis and secure boot, network and web protocols, cloud backends, mobile apps, and industrial and automotive topics. Each entry links out to an external tool, talk, paper, write-up or course with a short note. The README reports 900+ resources. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-connected-things-sec/))
- [Awesome Decomp Games](https://github.com/SubmitGame/awesome-decomp-games) — collection · game — A generated, rated index of public game reconstruction projects: decompilations, disassemblies, static recompilations, source ports, engine remakes and browser ports. A daily job merges several upstream lists and registries with GitHub and decomp.dev metadata, drops dead links and rebuilds the README and website from data files. Useful for discovery; its scores are automated heuristics, not reviews. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/submitgame-awesome-decomp-games/))
- [Awesome Executable Packing](https://github.com/packing-box/awesome-executable-packing) — collection · app, binary — A single-README list of resources on executable packing, meaning the bundling, compression, encryption, mutation, protection or virtualization of PE, ELF and other executables, including Android apps, and on detecting and unpacking packed files. About 770 entries are grouped into documentation, dated scientific papers, sample datasets, packers sorted by era from before 2000 to after 2010, and tools for packer identification, unpacking and binary analysis. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-executable-packing/))
- [Awesome Frida](https://github.com/dweinstein/awesome-frida) — collection · app, binary — An awesome list of libraries, scripts, tools, talks and articles built on or about Frida, the dynamic instrumentation toolkit, started by David Weinstein in May 2016. Its 82 entries fall into libraries (about half of them NowSecure Node.js modules), script collections and projects, talks and papers, tools powered by Frida such as r2frida, objection, friTap and Fridump, videos, blog posts and community links, most with a one-line summary. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-frida/))
- [Awesome Ghidra](https://github.com/AllsafeCyberSecurity/awesome-ghidra) — collection · binary — An awesome list of Ghidra scripts, plugins, extensions, learning materials and related services, kept by the Allsafe GitHub organization since December 2019. Its 49 entries comprise 36 scripts and extensions, from malware-analysis and crypto-constant finders to Go, JNI, EVM and EFI helpers, Python and Jupyter bridges, P-code emulation and patch-diff correlators; 10 books, talks and courses; and three related projects such as r2ghidra-dec and Ghidraaas, each with a one-line summary. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-ghidra/))
- [Awesome Malware Analysis](https://github.com/rshipp/awesome-malware-analysis) — collection · binary — A long-running single-README list of malware analysis tools and resources, grouped into 17 top-level sections covering open-source threat intelligence, detection and classification, online scanners and sandboxes, document and shellcode analysis, deobfuscation, debugging and reverse engineering, network, memory forensics, Windows artifacts, plus books and other references. Each entry is a name linked to an external tool or resource with a short description. The repository also keeps a Simplified Chinese translation of the list. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-malware-analysis/))
- [Awesome RE-MCP](https://github.com/crowdere/Awesome-RE-MCP) — collection · app, binary — A single-README list, written in July 2025, of 28 MCP servers for reverse-engineering tools, grouped into disassemblers and decompilers (IDA Pro, Ghidra, Binary Ninja and others), debuggers, static and dynamic analysis, network analysis, Android and security testing, with a one-line note per entry. It also names tools the author found without an MCP server, links four talks and guides, and points to starting material for writing new servers. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-re-mcp/))
- [Awesome REA Cases](https://github.com/songshikang0111/awesome-REA-cases) — collection · app, binary, firmware, game, protocol, website — A bilingual collection of REA and other AI-assisted reverse engineering cases, with source links and explicit distinctions between reported and reproduced outcomes. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-rea-cases/))
- [Awesome REA: Reverse Engineer Anything with AI (AIshihzong)](https://github.com/AIshihzong/awesome-reverse-engineer-anything) — collection · app, binary, game, protocol — An unofficial, bilingual awesome list of what AI agents have reverse-engineered and rebuilt, tagged by whether each case's own sources show that REA was used. It gathers REA's official resources and showcases; community cases in games, apps, hardware and protocols, and security evaluations; the wider wave of AI reverse engineering and clean-room rebuilds without confirmed REA; plugins, guides and research, related MCP tools, other lists and press; and a section on legal questions and controversy. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/aishihzong-awesome-reverse-engineer-anything/))
- [Awesome Reverse Engineering](https://github.com/morluto/awesome-reverse-engineering) — collection · app, binary, firmware, game, protocol, website — A task-oriented reverse-engineering resource list by REA's maintainer, useful for broader tools and foundations. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/morluto-awesome-re/))
- [Awesome UEFI Security](https://github.com/river-li/awesome-uefi-security) — collection · firmware — A single-README awesome list of UEFI and BIOS security material, which its maintainer describes as a personal and not comprehensive collection. It links UEFI, PI and ACPI specifications, EDK II development resources, firmware analysis tools such as UEFITool, efiXplorer, CHIPSEC and UEFI plugins for Ghidra and Binary Ninja, SMM-themed CTF challenges, analyses of bootkits found in the wild, vulnerability write-ups, conference talks, blog posts, papers and training courses. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-uefi-security/))
- [awesome-decompilation](https://github.com/nforest/awesome-decompilation) — collection · binary — A short, link-only reading list on decompiling machine code, last updated in 2023. Its 45 links cover foundational theses, compiler and program-analysis books, decompiler products and projects, pipeline topics from intermediate representations to type, control-flow and identifier recovery, early neural-decompilation papers and binary-to-source matching. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/nforest-awesome-decompilation/))
- [awesome-game-decompilations](https://github.com/CharlotteCross1998/awesome-game-decompilations) — collection · game — A single-README list of about 480 game decompilation, recompilation and disassembly projects, grouped by console from the NES and Game Boy to the Xbox 360, PC and mobile, with a large uncategorised section. Each entry is a game title linked to its project, mostly on GitHub, and entries with AI involvement are marked with a warning emoji under the maintainer’s disclosure policy. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-game-decompilations/))
- [awesome-mobile-security](https://github.com/vaib25vicky/awesome-mobile-security) — collection · app — A single-README list of Android and iOS application security resources, about 245 links in all, divided by platform into blogs, papers and how-tos, books, courses, tools, deliberately vulnerable lab apps, talks and miscellany. Android tools are further split into static analysis, dynamic analysis and online APK analyzers, and reverse-engineering entries such as smali/baksmali, radare2, Cutter, Bytecode Viewer, Frida and Objection sit alongside pentesting guides and vulnerability write-ups. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-mobile-security/))
- [awesome-rea (yibie): verification-focused projects and practice reports](https://github.com/yibie/awesome-rea) — collection · binary, game — Organizes AI-assisted game decompilation, native ports, static recompilation and tooling around stated acceptance checks, alongside a separate track of practitioner write-ups and discussions. Useful for finding primary reports and comparing how projects define correctness. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/yibie-awesome-rea/))
- [awesome-reverse-engineering (alphaSeclab)](https://github.com/alphaSeclab/awesome-reverse-engineering) — collection · app, binary — Large reverse-engineering link list, auto-exported in 2019 in English and Chinese, with abridged and full README versions. The full English README lists about 3,600 open-source tools and 2,300 posts and videos, grouped under Windows, Linux, Apple platforms and Android, and under tool sections for IDA, Ghidra, x64dbg, OllyDbg, WinDbg, radare2, Cuckoo, Binary Ninja and dynamic binary instrumentation. Tool rows carry a star count, language and upstream description captured at export time. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/alphaseclab-awesome-reverse-engineering/))
- [awesome-te](https://github.com/bnjreece/awesome-te) — collection · firmware, protocol — A public-domain directory, started in June 2026, of hacking projects for Teenage Engineering devices: custom firmware, reverse-engineering notes and protocol documentation, tools, hardware mods, teardown and recovery guides and community hubs for the OP-1, OP-Z, OP-XY, TP-7, SP-1 Stem Player, EP series, Pocket Operators and other gear. One-line entries are grouped by device and task, each with a maintainer-assigned active, dormant or archived marker, and the scope rule admits only released, TE-specific projects. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-te/))
- [awesome-unofficial-pc-ports](https://github.com/Sebastrion/awesome-unofficial-pc-ports) — collection · game — A single-README guide to unofficial PC ports of console games built from decompilations or recompilations and chosen for easy installation by players. Its 76 game entries, grouped under Nintendo, PlayStation, Xbox, SEGA, Indie and J2ME, each recommend a port with short setup notes and often link the underlying decompilation and alternative ports; a Utilities section adds two recompilation launchers. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/awesome-unofficial-pc-ports/))
- [Firmware-Analysis-Papers](https://github.com/onehouwong/Firmware-Analysis-Papers) — collection · firmware — A single-README reading list of 28 academic security papers on firmware and embedded-system analysis, grouped by year from 2013 to 2021. It covers rehosting and emulation, firmware fuzzing, symbolic execution, Bluetooth and baseband analysis and bug detection in Linux-based and bare-metal firmware. Each entry is a title linked to a PDF or publisher page, with its venue such as USENIX Security, NDSS, IEEE S&P or CCS. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/firmware-analysis-papers-list/))
- [Interactive IDA Plugin List](https://github.com/vmallet/ida-plugins) — collection · binary — A sortable, filterable list of 618 plugins, loaders, processor modules and scripts for Hex-Rays' IDA Pro, published as a GitHub Pages table with a static copy generated into the README. Each entry links to its project, almost always on GitHub, with a short description and, where recorded, its language (C++ or Python), category tags such as decompiler, deobfuscation, loader, debug or integration, and the date the project was last updated. The list grew out of onethawt's idaplugins-list of about 200 plugins. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/interactive-ida-plugin-list/))
- [OpenDeviceDB](https://github.com/opendevicedb/opendevicedb) — collection · firmware, protocol — OpenDeviceDB indexes devices with existing firmware or protocol reverse-engineering work and links back to source projects. Its website lists installation routes and remaining vendor dependencies, while importers aggregate existing databases and an MCP server lets agents query device records. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/opendevicedb/))
- [PRE-list](https://github.com/techge/PRE-list) — collection · protocol — A table-based reading list of 71 research papers on automatic protocol reverse engineering for network protocols, from the Protocol Informatics project (2004) to NetPlier (2021). Built from three surveys and extended since, it compares each method by approach, by input (network traces or execution traces) and output (message formats, state machines or other artifacts), and by the text, binary or hybrid protocols it was evaluated on, with full references and a short table of the few papers that released code. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/pre-list/))
- [RE list (extremecoders-re)](https://github.com/extremecoders-re/re-list) — collection · binary — A single-README list of 71 open-source reverse-engineering tools with a focus on binary analysis, kept as one alphabetical list from Amoco to X86isa. Each entry gives the tool's name, a link and a one- to three-sentence description; the entries span binary analysis frameworks, lifters to LLVM and other intermediate representations, symbolic execution and emulation engines, dynamic instrumentation, whole-system and virtual-machine introspection platforms, decompilers, debugger front ends and deobfuscation tools. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/extremecoders-re-list/))
- [Remade](https://github.com/replicraft/remade) — collection · app — The source repository of remade.wiki, a community-curated directory of open-source reimplementations of closed-source software, mostly creative, productivity and engineering applications. Each listing is a YAML record naming the original software, languages, platforms, license and self-reported status, compatibility and limitations, and the directory also tracks requests for software nobody has reimplemented yet. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/remade-reimplementation-directory/))
- [Reverse Engineering resources (wtsxDev)](https://github.com/wtsxDev/reverse-engineering) — collection · app, binary — A single-README awesome list, started in January 2017 and maintained under the KaliTut name, of 139 reverse-engineering links in 16 sections: books, courses, practice targets, hex editors, binary-format tools, disassemblers, binary analysis, bytecode analysis, import reconstruction, dynamic analysis, debugging, Mac decryption, document analysis, scripting, Android and YARA. Entries are mostly a tool or resource name with its link, a few with a one-line usage note, and lean towards Windows and macOS tooling and malware analysis. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/wtsxdev-reverse-engineering-list/))
- [Reversed with AI](https://reversedwithai.com/) — collection · app, binary, firmware, game, protocol — A moderated website directory of AI-assisted reverse-engineering projects across games, firmware, file formats, APIs and protocols, apps and legacy software. Each entry links to its primary source and records the author-reported model, tools, time, cost and outcome, with four checks for public source, reproduction steps, real-hardware testing and third-party confirmation. ([entry](https://awesomerea.org/resources/reversed-with-ai-directory/))

<a id="papers"></a>
## Papers

- [A Memorization Floor for LLM Refinement of Decompiled Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17236) (arXiv, 2026) — Studies whether LLM refinement of decompiled C recovers identifiers from input evidence or prior knowledge. Within-item ablations and output-matched chance baselines test naming gains on textbook and newly authored functions, including a second-model replication.
- [Agentic Reverse Engineering: From I/O to Code with Discovery Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15334) (NeurIPS 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/JiaruQian/IO2Code) — Synthesizes executable programs from input/output observations using LLM-guided evolutionary search, progressively revealed examples, simple-to-complex mutations and execution-error feedback. An active variant chooses inputs and queries a hidden reference program.
- [ASAP: Assembly-Source Aligned Pseudocode Refinement For Binary Decompilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06900) (arXiv, 2026) — ASAP aligns assembly and source representations during training, compresses assembly features into tokens, and supplies them alongside decompiler pseudocode to an LLM refiner. Masking and an assembly-advantage objective encourage the refiner to use low-level evidence.
- [Automatically Attacking Software Reverse Engineering AI Agents](https://papers.academic-conferences.org/index.php/eccws/article/view/4597) (ECCWS 2026, 2026) — A proof of concept for indirect prompt injection against Cline/GhidraMCP binary-analysis agents. An adapted AutoDAN generates embedded strings that make the agent describe different behavior. The authors demonstrate four small C programs with Qwen3-8B and transfer the examples to GPT-OSS-120B.
- [Between the Commits: Process, Error, and Claim Reliability in a Wholly AI-Authored Codebase](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.29744) (arXiv, 2026) — Studies development of the Extractor reverse-engineering tool through commits, available session logs and code-provenance tracing. Taxonomies separate instruction intent, change triggers and agent claims, exposing errors corrected before commits that ordinary repository history misses.
- [Beyond Text Matching: Towards Reference-Free Evaluation for Human-Oriented Binary Reverse Engineering](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3832783.3834339) (ASE 2026, 2026) — BinJudgeBench collects expert judgments for function naming, binary-code summaries and decompilation refinement. BinJudge learns to select a judge configuration for each input and task, evaluating agreement with human scores rather than executable correctness.
- [Binary Decompilation LLM with Feedback-Driven Multi-Turn Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16162) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://zenodo.org/records/23120286) — AutoDecompiler trains function-level decompilation models to revise assembly- or pseudocode-derived outputs using compilation, execution and I/O feedback. Its reinforcement-learning design combines diagnostic feedback with rewards for valid code, tested behavior, syntax and data-flow structure.
- [CASCADE: LLM-powered JavaScript Deobfuscator at Google](https://doi.org/10.1145/3786583.3786873) (ICSE-SEIP 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/google/jsir) — Uses Gemini to locate the prelude functions that Obfuscator.IO inserts for string obfuscation, then applies constant-propagation and inlining passes in the JSIR compiler IR to restore string literals and API names deterministically.
- [CHISEL-ing Back Source Code with AI-enabled Iterative Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27981) (SURE @ ACM CCS 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/VarunKohli18/chisel) — CHISEL refines Ghidra pseudocode using compiler diagnostics and type-aware differential fuzzing against the original function. It retains counterexamples and candidate history; existing I/O pairs are held out for evaluation rather than supplied to the recovery loop.
- [CrackMeBench: Binary Reverse Engineering for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10597) (arXiv, 2026) — Evaluates agents on executable-checked CrackMe tasks: infer validation logic from binaries and submit an accepted input or artifact, while recording tool use, time, and scoring attempts.
- [CREBench: Evaluating Large Language Models in Cryptographic Binary Reverse Engineering](https://openreview.net/forum?id=tLYdf8hWJB) (COLM 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/wangyu-ovo/CREBench) — Benchmarks cryptographic binary reverse engineering across algorithm recognition, key or IV extraction, wrapper reconstruction, and flag recovery using 432 controlled challenges.
- [Cyber-Zero: Training Cybersecurity Agents without Runtime](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2026/hash/f9f54762cbb4fe4dbffdd4f792c31221-Abstract-Conference.html) (ICLR 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/amazon-science/Cyber-Zero) — Cyber-Zero synthesizes training trajectories for capture-the-flag (CTF) agents from public writeups instead of running the challenges. Writeups collected from CTFtime through the CTF Archives repository are cleaned, filtered and completed with DeepSeek-V3-0324, which fills in missing metadata and extracts flags; after removing 47 challenges that overlap the evaluation benchmarks, the corpus holds 6,188 writeups for 4,610 challenges from 543 competitions in six categories, 2017–2025. Two DeepSeek-V3-0324 personas then simulate each solve: a CTF player that sees only the challenge content, and a Bash terminal that sees the writeup and flag, invents plausible command output and may inject hints, which are removed before training. Trajectories use the EnIGMA scaffold format and are kept only if they reach the exact flag, pass format checks and are judged by an LLM filter to follow the writeup's approach. Qwen3, Qwen2.5-Instruct and SWE-agent-LM models are fine-tuned on 9,464 of these trajectories and evaluated with EnIGMA+, the authors' parallel version of EnIGMA, on InterCode-CTF, NYU CTF Bench and Cybench. The fine-tuned Qwen3-32B (Cyber-Zero-32B) rises from 20.3% to 33.4% average Pass@1, the 13.1-point gain reported in the abstract.
- [Dashboard2Code: Evaluating Multimodal Models on Reconstructing Interactive Dashboards](https://aclanthology.org/2026.acl-long.1750/) (ACL 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/nth2000/Dashboard2Code) — Evaluates agents that actively explore a running dashboard and reconstruct its appearance and interaction logic. DashboardMimic provides 180 Plotly+Dash examples, with code-semantic analysis and dynamic interaction tests for the generated implementations.
- [Decaf: Improving Neural Decompilation with Automatic Feedback and Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11501) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/AlexShypula/decaf) — Decaf samples multiple candidate decompilations, recompiles them and ranks them using compiler-derived byte distance or a learned reranker. The work studies candidate selection as a way to improve neural decompilation without an iterative source-repair conversation.
- [Decoding What iOS Location Services Send to Apple: An LLM Is an Unreliable Analyst but a Useful Toolmaker](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21597) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/doug-leith/ios-protobuf-decoding) — Compares direct LLM recovery of Apple PBCodable schemas with an AI-written Extractor checked against compiled synthetic fixtures, published descriptors and captured traffic. It shows how plausible recovered schemas can silently decode values incorrectly.
- [Decompiling Quantum Assembly into Structured Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05012) (arXiv, 2026) — Quelle recovers library calls, loops, functions and symbolic parameters from compiled OpenQASM. It removes routing artifacts, proposes structured reconstructions and checks them against circuit semantics, retaining gate-level fallbacks when recovery fails.
- [Decompiling the Synergy: An Empirical Study of Human–LLM Teaming in Software Reverse Engineering](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/decompiling-the-synergy-an-empirical-study-of-human-llm-teaming-in-software-reverse-engineering/) (NDSS 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/mahaloz/dec-synergy-study) — Studies how human analysts work with LLM assistance during static software reverse engineering. A survey of 153 practitioners informed six LLM features built into an instrumented IDA Pro plugin. Then 48 participants (24 self-reported novices and 24 experts) analyzed two CTF-style binaries, with the features enabled on one randomly assigned challenge. From graded writeups, interaction logs and surveys, the authors report 18 findings: novice understanding rose by about 98% to expert levels while experts gained little, known-algorithm functions were triaged up to 2.4× faster, and artifact recovery rose by at least 66%, alongside harmful hallucinations.
- [Decoupling is a Necessity: Transformation-Agnostic Decompiled Code Recovery under Optimization and Obfuscation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27889) (arXiv, 2026) — ReSource refines decompiled functions affected by compiler optimization and obfuscation. Its stages retrieve guidance from a Semantic Distortion Database, predict a control-flow skeleton, and refine names and formatting. Evaluation measures code similarity and retrieval of the corresponding original function from a source corpus.
- [Does It Render Everywhere? A Study of Cross-Environment Compatibility in MLLM-Generated Webpages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12518) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/ZiyunGuo/WebCompat) — WebCompat evaluates screenshot-reconstructed webpages across browsers and devices. Its XCompat detector combines rendered DOM geometry, viewport metadata and screenshots to identify layout and element failures, independently of similarity to the original design.
- [Echo: Learning-based Matching Decompilation using Trusted Back Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18706) (arXiv, 2026) — Echo iteratively generates source and compiler configurations, recompiles candidates, and uses assembly differences to guide configuration search and code refinement. It evaluates matching on function benchmarks and selected functions from a Mirai binary.
- [Faultless: A Program Equivalence Technique for Validating and Evaluating Neural Decompilers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34089) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/squaresLab/faultless) — Compares neural-decompiler C output with deterministic decompilation or reference functions using symbolic execution and a specialized memory model. It supports both analyst-facing validation and model evaluation without requiring the surrounding codebase.
- [FidelityGPT: Correcting Decompilation Distortions with Retrieval Augmented Generation](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/fidelitygpt-correcting-decompilation-distortions-with-retrieval-augmented-generation/) (NDSS 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/ZhouZhiping045/FidelityGPT) — FidelityGPT post-processes existing decompiler output: an LLM labels lines with six distortion types, such as non-conventional dereferencing, redundant variables and confusing control flow, and then rewrites the labeled lines. Its prompts define the distortion types and add retrieval-augmented context from a manually annotated database of distorted IDA Pro and Ghidra lines. A semantic-intensity algorithm chooses which lines to query, and a variable-dependency analysis carries redundant-variable context across 50-line chunks. On 620 function pairs, the authors report 89% detection accuracy and 83% precision, and correction fix rates of 94% (FR) and 64% (CFR) against 83% and 37% for their DeGPT runs.
- [FirmCure:Towards Autonomous and Adaptive Rehosting of Linux-Based Firmware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24549) (arXiv, 2026) — FirmCure combines static dependency analysis, iterative boot-configuration repair and runtime fault diagnosis to rehost Linux firmware in QEMU. Specialized agents apply targeted repairs and use execution feedback to recover service behavior.
- [FirmPilot: Evidence-Guided Multi-Agent Environment Recovery for IoT Firmware Rehosting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14903) (arXiv, 2026) — FirmPilot reconstructs QEMU execution environments through coordinated retrieval, planning, filesystem, persistent-state and network recovery. Proposed changes are applied as constrained artifact deltas, then checked by rerunning the firmware and fixed probes.
- [Fool Me If You Can: On the Robustness of Binary Code Similarity Detection Models against Semantics-Preserving Transformations](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3797116) (Proceedings of the ACM on Software Engineering (FSE 2026), 2026) — The paper builds asmFooler to test whether learned binary code similarity detection (BCSD) models keep matching functions after semantics-preserving changes. From 620 baseline x86-64 ELF binaries (coreutils, binutils, PuTTY, nginx, SPEC2006 and others, built with Clang at O0–O3), it generates 9,565 variants using in-place instruction rewriting, basic-block reordering, Obfuscator-LLVM, semantic NOPs and junk code, and it adds a greedy search for dead-code sequences that make dissimilar functions look similar. Across Genius, Gemini, Asm2Vec, SAFE, Trex and BinShot, robustness depends on each model's preprocessing, architecture and features: block reordering drops BinShot's recall from 0.972 to 0.036 but lowers it by under 0.05 for the CFG-based Genius and Gemini. The false-positive attack reaches up to 100% success with 14.75 inserted instructions on average.
- [From Code to Requirements: Agentic Reverse Engineering of Business Rules at Enterprise Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22719) (arXiv, 2026) — Uses seven agents and human review to recover business requirements from source, tests, configuration and documentation. A reported enterprise migration checks extracted rules against production-defect records.
- [From Dead Code and Static Requirements to Working Engines: Software Revival with Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36161) (arXiv, 2026) — ReviveBench evaluates repair of nonrunning software and source-unavailable implementation of bounded computational and transactional engines from open specifications, using hidden reference-based checks. It also audits grading defects that can wrongly accept or reject reconstructions.
- [From Obfuscated to Obvious: A Comprehensive JavaScript Deobfuscation Tool for Security Analysis](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/from-obfuscated-to-obvious-a-comprehensive-javascript-deobfuscation-tool-for-security-analysis/) (NDSS 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/XingTuLab/JSIMPLIFIER) — JSimplifier recovers readable JavaScript from obfuscated scripts in three stages. A preprocessor normalizes malformed or mixed-encoding input; a deobfuscator combines AST-based static simplification with selective execution of obfuscated call sites in a Node.js vm context, feeding the results back into the AST; and an optional humanizer renames identifiers with an LLM, adapting HumanifyJS, then formats the output with Prettier. The authors catalog 20 obfuscation techniques in four categories and assemble MalJS (23,212 malicious scripts) and BenignJS (21,209 scripts). They report that JSimplifier processes all 44,421 samples and reduces Halstead length by 88.2% on 1,296 multi-technique JsDeObsBench samples.
- [From Visual Widgets to UI Code: Efficient Tool-Grounded Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12611) (MUCG @ ECCV 2026, 2026) — WidgetGen reconstructs widget screenshots as React JSX. OCR text boxes and color palettes guide whole-widget layout reasoning and optional chart generation before one terminal render; generated render/code pairs also support model fine-tuning.
- [GameReplica: A Benchmark for Black-Box Visual Game Replication by Vision-Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22308) (arXiv, 2026) — Evaluates whether vision-language agents can infer hidden game rules through screenshots and active interaction, then build runnable single-file HTML replicas across 125 tasks from 25 web games, each with five difficulty levels.
- [HELIOS: Hierarchical Graph Abstraction for Structure-Aware LLM Decompilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.14598) (arXiv, 2026) — HELIOS gives an LLM a hierarchical summary of binary control flow and function calls alongside decompiler output, with optional compiler feedback. It evaluates compilability and functional correctness.
- [Humans vs. Machines: A Comparison With AI Agents in Software Reverse Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14960) (arXiv, 2026) — Compares human and LLM-agent understanding and navigation on the same static reverse-engineering challenges, then studies how binary representations consume context windows.
- [IChart2Code: Benchmarking Multimodal Large Language Models for Interactive Chart Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32413) (arXiv, 2026) — Benchmarks reconstruction of interactive charts from a reference screenshot, local data and specified actions. Its TRAIL framework uses Developer and Inspector agents with browser interaction traces to diagnose errors and revise the HTML/JavaScript implementation.
- [Idioms: A Simple and Effective Framework for Turbo-Charging Local Neural Decompilation with Well-Defined Types](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/idioms-a-simple-and-effective-framework-for-turbo-charging-local-neural-decompilation-with-well-defined-types/) (NDSS 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/squaresLab/idioms) — Idioms fine-tunes code language models to turn Hex-Rays output for one target function into predicted original C together with definitions of the user-defined structs, unions and typedefs it uses. Input can include neighboring decompiled functions in breadth-first call-graph order, so evidence about a type scattered across callers and callees reaches the model. The authors also build Realtype, 157,163 functions from compiled open-source C repositories with full type definitions. Their CodeGemma-7b-based model passes ExeBench unit tests for 54.4% of functions versus 46.3% for LLM4Decompile and 37.5% for Nova, and scores at least 95% higher on Realtype.
- [IWR-Bench: Can LVLMs reconstruct interactive webpage from a user interaction video?](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2026/hash/55bfedfd31489e5ae83c9ce8eec7b0e1-Abstract-Conference.html) (ICLR 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/SIGMME/IWR-Bench) — Tests reconstruction of interactive webpages from user-interaction videos and supplied static assets. Its 113 tasks replay annotated action sequences to assess visual fidelity and functional behavior across observed page states.
- [Khost: KVM-based Near Native MCU Firmware Rehosting](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity26/presentation/wang-chunlin) (USENIX Security '26, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/seclab-fudan/Khost) — Extends KVM with MCU instruction handling, memory mapping and interrupt modeling to rehost firmware on ARMv8-A hosts. An MMIO monitor and firmware wrapper support peripheral interaction and coverage-guided testing.
- [Lifting Optimized Binaries to Canonical Compiler IR via Structure-Aware Retrieval and Iterative Verification](https://aclanthology.org/2026.acl-long.527/) (ACL 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/Azhu-c/BRIDGE-rag) — BRIDGE lifts stripped, optimized x86-64 and ARM64 functions, given as llvm-objdump disassembly, to LLVM IR. Its target is the unoptimized IR that a fixed clang -O0 configuration emits for the original source. Offline, it builds a retrieval database from 69,253 ExeBench functions compiled at -O0 to -O3, using LLVM pseudo-probe instrumentation to align assembly fragments with the matching -O0 IR regions. At inference it splits each function's angr-recovered control-flow graph into control-flow segments such as loops and if–else regions, retrieves the three nearest aligned assembly–IR pairs per segment from a FAISS index of Nova-1.3B embeddings, and prompts a hosted general-purpose LLM (DeepSeek-chat) to write the IR. Up to five refinement rounds then feed LLVM verifier and compiler diagnostics and unit-test failures back to the model. On HumanEval-Decompile and MBPP compiled with Clang 19 at -O0 to -O3, average re-executability is 65.2% and 65.4% on x86-64 and 45.6% and 42.1% on ARM64. The rule-based MCTOLL reaches 62.1% and 64.9% on x86-64, and the five directly prompted API LLMs reach at most 21.5%.
- [LLM Agent-Assisted Reverse Engineering with Quantitative Readability Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06838) (arXiv, 2026) — Uses a composite Quantitative Readability Score (QRS) to guide agents refining decompiled C. Lexical familiarity, structural simplicity and coding idioms are combined with a binary-structure similarity gate. Experiments compare refinement with and without shell access on 210 simple C binaries.
- [Locate, Then Reason: End-to-End Patch Presence Testing in Stripped Binaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25071) (arXiv, 2026) — Locates patch-relevant code in stripped binaries using instruction-aligned retrieval and caller/callee context, then uses an LLM to screen candidate matches and compare patch-specific behavior with vulnerable and patched references.
- [MirrorCode: AI can rebuild entire programs from behavior alone](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30182) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/epoch-research/MirrorCode) — Tests reimplementation of 25 command-line programs using execute-only reference access, documentation and visible examples. Separate scoring environments compare exact outputs on visible and held-out end-to-end tests.
- [NotDec: WebAssembly Decompilation With Inter-Procedural Type Recovery](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3744916.3787762) (ICSE 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/NotDec/NotDec) — NotDec lifts WebAssembly into SSA-based IR, uses Retypd-derived type constraints and pointer/value distinctions to recover structures, and emits C. Its study separates full interprocedural analysis from a faster intraprocedural variant.
- [On the Viability of Requirements Generation from Code: An Experience Report](https://doi.org/10.1109/REW72749.2026.00023) (IEEE REW 2026 / AIRE, 2026) — Studies a RAG-based, human-supervised pipeline for recovering requirements and code traces from two projects. It also tests deliberate generation of unimplemented requirements and requirements smells (potential quality issues), exposing verification and labeling difficulties.
- [POZZER: A Power Side Channel-guided Fuzzer for Black-Box Embedded Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23583) (arXiv, 2026) — Builds a noise-tolerant pseudo execution-flow graph from one power trace per test input, using newly observed behavior as LibAFL feedback. It explores embedded firmware without access to its binary, source or instrumentation.
- [PrismaCoder: Pushing the Boundaries of Open Multimodal Code Intelligence through Decomposed Rubric Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20538) (arXiv, 2026) — Trains visual-code models using separate code and rendered-output rubrics for data filtering and reinforcement learning. Relevant evaluations include rebuilding webpages, charts and vector graphics from images.
- [ProgramBench: Can Language Models Rebuild Programs From Scratch?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.03546) (NeurIPS 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/facebookresearch/ProgramBench) — Evaluates black-box program reconstruction across 200 tasks: agents query an executable and read its documentation, then independently implement a codebase whose behavior is checked by generated tests.
- [ProgramDistill: From Interactive Web Apps to Verifiable Reference-Guided SWE Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18805) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://huggingface.co/datasets/microsoft/ProgramDistill) — Evaluates recovery of web-app behavior from a live reference with hidden source. It provides 4,063 feature-restoration tasks across 26 applications and a separate 12-application reconstruction setting, scored by replaying dependent browser workflows.
- [Protocol Reverse Engineering via Deep Transfer Learning](https://doi.org/10.1145/3797124) (Proceedings of the ACM on Software Engineering / FSE 2026, 2026) — Transfers field-boundary patterns from standardized protocol traces labeled by Tshark to poorly documented target protocols. A source-selection stage chooses related protocols, and a neural domain-adaptation model infers message syntax from their shared patterns.
- [Reactive Peripheral Modeling for Faithful Firmware Rehosting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29737) (arXiv, 2026) — Bluezz models peripherals as stateful event-condition-action systems, coordinating interrupts, MMIO and DMA rather than treating accesses independently. It uses these models to rehost BLE firmware and reach connected protocol states for analysis and fuzzing.
- [Recall-Oriented Precise Binary-to-Source Function Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23546) (arXiv, 2026) — BScout matches decompiled functions to a source corpus using chunked embeddings and inlining-aware training. Existing matches provide positional clues to expand candidates; LLM selection and structural filters refine the resulting correspondences.
- [Recovering Process Variables from Industrial Network Traffic via Search-Based Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16403) (CCS 2026, 2026) — PVParser recovers process-variable layouts from industrial traffic and historian records. Periodic-pattern detection locates relevant payloads; a modified Monte Carlo tree search proposes field boundaries, types and endianness, then aligns decoded sequences with observed process variables.
- [RevengeBench: Reverse Engineering Code-Space Policies from Behavioral Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26094) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/bethgelab/revenge-bench) — Tests whether agents can recover executable decision policies from behavioral traces and self-designed opponent probes, using 75 synthetic policies across five game arenas and measuring held-out action agreement and downstream competitive value.
- [Reveree: Diagnosing LLM Reverse-Engineering Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01185) (arXiv, 2026) — Evaluates reverse-engineering agent traces through flag recovery, eight analysis milestones and action profiles. Deterministic checks and an outcome-blinded LLM judge help locate failures beyond a single solve-rate score.
- [RFCLLM: Evaluating LLMs' Reasoning Ability of Network Protocol State Machines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13389) (Findings of EMNLP 2026, 2026) · [code](https://anonymous.4open.science/r/rfcllm) — Tests reasoning over protocol state machines using 1,482 questions across 16 protocols. Four context settings distinguish RFC retrieval from reasoning over supplied transitions; tasks cover next states, conditions, predecessor states and paths.
- [SatBleed: Security of Commoditized Communication Modules in Satellites](https://doi.org/10.1109/SP63933.2026.00213) (IEEE S&P 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/EmsecCispa/SatBleed) — Studies satellite communication modules through hardware inspection, firmware reverse engineering, protocol analysis, partial rehosting and targeted testing. A component-level threat model connects recovered data flows to security boundaries.
- [SCDBench: A Benchmark for LLM-Based Smart Contract Decompilers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29059) (arXiv, 2026) — Benchmarks LLM-based EVM decompilation on 600 source-verified Ethereum Solidity contracts, sampled in easy, medium and hard thirds from 772,736 unique bytecode–source pairs after near-duplicate filtering. Each task supplies EVM assembly and optional four-byte selector hints and asks for self-contained Solidity with compiler settings. Outputs are scored in four cumulative stages: format completeness, compilation, ABI recovery, and semantic consistency, where 227,383 recorded test cases for 14,553 public functions are replayed on the recompiled output. With one compiler-feedback repair call, GPT-5.3-Codex compiled 90.3% of contracts, while the best semantic results were 29.4% of functions and 42 of 600 contracts recovered perfectly, both by Claude Opus 4.7.
- [Schema: Discovering Unknown Environments via Agentic Program Induction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39140) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/guanning03/Schema) — Infers executable models of game state and transition rules from interaction. Agents revise these models against recorded history, search within them, and compare predicted observations with real outcomes while executing plans.
- [ScreenCoder: Advancing Visual-to-Code Generation for Front-End Automation via Modular Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22827) (EMNLP 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/leigest519/ScreenCoder) — Splits screenshot-to-code generation among grounding, planning and generation agents that label UI regions, build a hierarchical layout tree and write HTML/CSS. The pipeline also produces the Screen-10K training pairs used for supervised and reinforcement fine-tuning, and the paper introduces the 1,000-page ScreenBench.
- [Semantic Validation of Packer Identification Tools: Characterization, Repair, and Downstream Impact](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25923) (arXiv, 2026) — Uses unpacking outcomes as reference evidence to assess packer labels, normalizes heterogeneous tool results, and evaluates complementary signature/heuristic pools with static predicates derived from unpacker implementations. It traces how identification errors affect later unpacking and malware classification.
- [ShieldedCode: Learning Robust Representations for Virtual Machine Protected Code](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2026/hash/286976486c9bbfa2c2069b01b259b19d-Abstract-Conference.html) (ICLR 2026, 2026) — ShieldedCode studies software protection from the defender's side: it trains code language models on pairs of C source functions and their virtualization-protected (VMP) x86-64 code so that the models can generate, compare and rank protected variants. Training pairs come from AnghaBench functions compiled at O0–O3, protected at levels L0–L3 by a commercial VMP tool, then disassembled and normalized. The model adds attention masks for dependencies within, between adjacent and across virtual instructions, contrastive objectives for functional equivalence and protection strength, and a protection-effectiveness task that ranks variants of the same source. Continual pre-training starts from CodeLlama 34B and adds The Stack to the corpus, and fine-tuning uses 2.5 million source–VM pairs. On HumanEval-compile, VM code generation reaches 26.95% Pass@1 at level L0 (GPT-4o: 22.58%) and 14.71% at L3. On BinaryCorp-VirtualAssembly similarity detection, Recall@1 for the O0+L1 setting is 0.488, against 0.333 for jTrans with a linear probe. The authors also run a manual reverse-engineering study and pattern-matching and angr attacks on the generated protection.
- [SK2Decompile: LLM-based Two-Phase Binary Decompilation from Skeleton to Skin](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2026/hash/9132583dd869acb0cafeb0ed25eb031a-Abstract-Conference.html) (ICLR 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/albertan017/LLM4Decompile/tree/85b364bf093eb2ee4f3687cfe38a203fca89f23e/sk2decompile) — Splits LLM decompilation into two phases: a Structure Recovery model turns decompiler pseudocode into an identifier-free IR that keeps control flow and data structures, trained with compiler-based RL rewards, and an Identifier Naming model then restores names under a semantic-similarity reward. The authors report a 21.6% average re-executability gain over GPT-5-mini on HumanEval and a 29.4% average R2I improvement over Idioms on GitHub2025.
- [SoK: A Large-Scale Empirical Study of Emulation-Based Dynamic Analysis Research for ARM Cortex-M Firmware (Extended Version)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38872) (arXiv (extended version), 2026) · [code](https://github.com/IoTS-P/LargeRehostingTesting) — Organizes 24 MCU analysis tools into configuration recovery, emulation, fuzzing and diagnosis stages, then evaluates the runnable, pipeline-compatible subset on 4,571 filtered Cortex-M firmware images. Studies where recovered configurations and emulation artifacts fail to support later analysis.
- [SoK: AI-Augmented Binary Reversing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17398) (arXiv, 2026) — A systematization-of-knowledge survey of machine-learning, LLM and agentic approaches to binary reversing. Version 2 collects 246 papers published since 2015, found through keyword searches of 11 security, software-engineering and AI venues plus citation snowballing, and organizes them into 22 domains by inference task: from code-boundary detection and recovery of function names, types and struct layouts to code similarity, malware and vulnerability analysis, and decompilation. Its taxonomy links conventional triage, static, dynamic and security-testing analyses to AI pipelines through the binary-derived artifacts they exchange, and it derives 12 insights and open directions such as binary-aligned ground truth and goal-driven agentic reversing.
- [SoK: Are LLMs Reliable at Source Code Recovery? A Taxonomy and Empirical Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11556) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://anonymous.4open.science/r/decompbench-65CB/) — This SoK organizes LLM-assisted source-recovery designs and separates compilation, readability, structural and behavioral metrics. Its controlled study varies architecture, compiler optimization and symbol availability, with smaller ablations for input representation, model roles and source language.
- [SPRITE: From Static Mockups to Engine-Ready Game UI](https://doi.org/10.1145/3772363.3798742) (CHI EA 2026 (poster/extended abstract), 2026) — Transforms game-interface screenshots into editable engine assets through hierarchical YAML, visual grounding, asset extraction, and Unity UXML/USS synthesis, with evaluation on a curated game-UI benchmark and review by three senior UI/UX designers.
- [Stage-Supervised Latent Reasoning for Single-Shot JavaScript Deobfuscation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27058) (arXiv, 2026) — Uses intermediate Webcrack rewrites to supervise Coconut-style latent reasoning for JavaScript deobfuscation. Training exposes staged code transformations; inference receives obfuscated code and generates a cleaned program in one shot.
- [State-Aware Generation of Deployable Websites from Visual Prototypes and Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17861) (arXiv, 2026) — VisStateCoder links visual prototype elements, project files, interaction requirements and render evidence in a project state graph, then uses those links to generate websites and limit subsequent repairs to selected files.
- [STILL: Recovering Lowered STL Semantics for LLM-assisted C++ Decompilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26408) (arXiv, 2026) — STILL predicts function-level STL container presence from stripped control-flow graphs and turns those predictions into compact hints for LLM refinement of Ghidra output. StlBench supplies controlled labels for five container classes.
- [SWE-Game: Can Coding Agents Build the Games We Want?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33678) (arXiv, 2026) — Evaluates construction, completion, repair and Godot-to-Unity porting against executable reference games. Evaluator-owned probes and input replays check gameplay behavior, while visual rubrics assess presentation separately.
- [TaoD2C-Bench: Benchmarking MLLMs for Industrial UI Code Generation Beyond Visual Fidelity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10374) (arXiv, 2026) — TaoD2C-Bench reconstructs interfaces from design images, layer metadata and component-library documentation. It separates requirement inference from implementation, checking component choices and layout relations through source/runtime evidence alongside rendered appearance.
- [The CodeInverter Suite: Structure- and Data-Aware Binary Decompilation with Efficient LLMs](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3832783.3837554) (ASE 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/LiuPeiP-CS/CodeInverter) — CodeInverter studies function-level LLM decompilation using control-flow graphs and memory-to-data mappings extracted with IDA/objdump. It presents a prompt workflow, training corpus and model family, evaluated through compilation, benchmark input/output tests and edit similarity.
- [The Deception Delta: Adversarial Evaluation of LLM-Based Smart Contract Bytecode Forensics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14098) (ACM CCS 2026, 2026) — Evaluates how language models interpret raw EVM bytecode from purpose-built deceptive and control contracts. It distinguishes recognizing a hidden transfer mechanism from correctly judging its risk, using model judges alongside judge-independent checks.
- [The Evolution of Binary Decompilation in the Modern Era: A Taxonomy, Literature Review, and Future Perspectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24955) (arXiv, 2026) — This survey maps traditional, dynamic, matching and neural decompilation approaches, then organizes type recovery, control-flow restructuring, naming and refinement tasks alongside evaluation metrics and benchmark choices. An accompanying repository provides the reviewed literature list.
- [The Next Challenge for Agentic Cybersecurity: A Realistic, Contamination-Free Reverse Engineering Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11469) (arXiv, 2026) — Introduces SRE-Bench, with privately authored programs and protected binary variants, to study agent recovery of program semantics while reducing training-data contamination.
- [Towards LLM-Resistant Software Protection: Agent Failure Patterns in CTF Reverse Engineering](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/auto-draft-657/) (Workshop on Binary Analysis Research (BAR) 2026, 2026) — Runs Claude Code, Codex CLI and ChatGPT-5.2 Pro on 24 crackme-style challenges selected from 15 international CTF events held in 2025, and attributes their failures to stages of an Observe–Comprehend–Plan loop and to three protection effects: concealment, complication and misdirection. From the failure logs, the authors derive four recurring weaknesses (training bias, over-trust in observations, context limitation and plan persistence). They rerun both CLI agents without a decompiler and compare outcomes with in-competition solve rates.
- [Training Language Model Agents to Find Vulnerabilities with CTF-Dojo](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v306/zhuo26c.html) (ICML 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/amazon-science/CTF-Dojo) — CTF-Dojo is an execution environment for training LLM agents on capture-the-flag tasks: 658 challenges from 50 competitions held between 2011 and 2025, taken from pwn.college's CTF Archive and containerized with Docker. Its CTF-Forge pipeline prompts DeepSeek-V3-0324 to write each challenge's Dockerfile, docker-compose file and challenge metadata; across three independent runs, 650 challenges consistently built and answered on their expected ports. Qwen3-Coder and DeepSeek-V3-0324 agents in the EnIGMA+ scaffold, capped at 40 turns, collected trajectories. Matched CTFtime writeups served as hints during collection and were removed afterwards, and ports, paths, distractor code and other runtime details were randomized per rollout. Rejection-sampling fine-tuning of Qwen3 8B, 14B and 32B on 486 flag-capturing trajectories from 274 solved challenges raises average Pass@1 on InterCode-CTF, NYU CTF Bench and Cybench by up to 11.6 points; the 32B model reaches 31.9%.
- [Tycho: Active Abstraction with Programmatic World Models for ARC-AGI-3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28287) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/NIMI-research/Tycho) — Tycho constructs editable Python models of game state, transitions, rendering and outcomes from action/observation histories in unfamiliar ARC-AGI-3 games. It checks model predictions against recorded transitions and can use, repair or bypass these models during planning.
- [UI2App: Benchmarking Visual Interaction Inference in Executable Web Application Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06306) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/chenmancm169/UI2App) — UI2App evaluates runnable React/TypeScript applications generated from 600 screenshots across 95 web applications. It separates build executability, visual fidelity and screenshot-implied interaction behavior, including cross-route state, using a reference interaction taxonomy and public evaluation pipeline.
- [UniCoder: Unified Visual-to-Code Generation via Symbolic Rewards and Reference-Guided Code Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31732) (arXiv, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/JimmyZhengyz/unicoder) — UniCoder trains a visual-to-code model for charts, SVGs and webpages. Its reinforcement-learning objective combines rendering checks, image similarity and code-derived visual attributes; low-reward training groups receive reference-code trajectories to improve exploration.
- [User-Space Dependency-Aware Rehosting for Linux-Based Firmware Binaries](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/user-space-dependency-aware-rehosting-for-linux-based-firmware-binaries/) (NDSS 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/qc9c/Firmwell) — FIRMWELL reconstructs the execution environment of Linux firmware services by running their initialization processes, locating blocking dependencies and repairing missing system-level behavior. It combines containerized QEMU-user emulation with a QEMU-system fallback, using process traces and binary analysis to recover resources needed by the target service.
- [Vision2Web: A Hierarchical Benchmark for Visual Website Development with Agent Verification](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v306/he26n.html) (ICML 2026, 2026) · [code](https://github.com/zai-org/Vision2Web) — Benchmarks website development from visual prototypes and written requirements across 100 static-page, 66 interactive-frontend and 27 full-stack tasks, combining workflow-based functional checks with model-judged visual comparison.
- [WebWorld: The Browser as a World Model for Self-Improving Web Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30530) (arXiv, 2026) — Uses typed browser interaction contracts to check progress and preserve previously verified capabilities while improving generated web code. Only accepted transitions become training examples.
- [When LLM Decompilers Recompile More and Preserve Less](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05370) (arXiv, 2026) — Decompile-Diverge builds function-level behavioral comparisons from source-derived drivers and fuzzed reference inputs. The study compares reconstructed functions on the same inputs and examines divergences and missing crashes that compilation and supplied tests can miss.
- [Beyond Classification: Inferring Function Names in Stripped Binaries via Domain Adapted LLMs](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/beyond-classification-inferring-function-names-in-stripped-binaries-via-domain-adapted-llms/) (NDSS 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/OSUSecLab/SymGen) — SymGen infers function names for stripped binaries by generating them with a LoRA-fine-tuned Code Llama model instead of classifying over a fixed name vocabulary. Ghidra decompiles each stripped function, its own name is masked, and callee names are normalized for deduplication. Before fine-tuning, the model is domain-adapted on decompiled code paired with LLM-generated summaries of the matching source functions. On functions from 33 C projects built for x86-64, x86-32, ARM and MIPS at O0–O3, deduplicated from 2,237,915 to 495,494, the authors report average word-level F1 of 0.277 (0.380 on x86-64), plus case studies on obfuscated binaries and Linux malware.
- [BinMetric: A Comprehensive Binary Code Analysis Benchmark for Large Language Models](https://www.ijcai.org/proceedings/2025/858) (IJCAI 2025, 2025) — BinMetric poses 1,000 questions across six binary-analysis tasks: call-site reconstruction, decompilation, signature recovery, binary code summarization, algorithm classification and assembly instruction generation. The questions come from 20 open-source C projects compiled for x86-64, stripped and processed with IDA Pro, and aligned with source code through DWARF information. The authors evaluate 12 LLMs from five model families with one-shot prompts, alongside IDA Pro’s decompiler, LLM4Decompile, BinT5 and HexT5 where those apply.
- [Cybench: A Framework for Evaluating Cybersecurity Capabilities and Risks of Language Models](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2025/hash/3e9412a9c1d93810ef3ef7825115016b-Abstract-Conference.html) (ICLR 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/andyzorigin/cybench) — Cybench turns 40 professional-level capture-the-flag tasks from four competitions (HackTheBox Cyber Apocalypse 2024, SekaiCTF 2022–23, GlacierCTF and HKCert) into runnable agent tasks. Each task has a description, starter files, a Kali Linux container for the agent with task servers in Docker, and the time the first human team needed to solve it, from 2 minutes to 24 hours 54 minutes. Each task is also split into subtasks that grade intermediate steps. The authors evaluate eight models with a structured bash agent and compare four scaffolds for GPT-4o and Claude 3.5 Sonnet. Without subtask guidance, the best agents solved only tasks whose first human solve took at most 11 minutes, and Claude 3.5 Sonnet reached 17.5% unguided success.
- [D-LiFT: Improving LLM-based Decompiler Backend via Code Quality-driven Fine-tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10125) (arXiv, 2025) — D-LIFT uses D-Score to guide LLM fine-tuning and output selection, improving decompiled code while prioritizing accuracy. It evaluates Ghidra output from coreutils and util-linux.
- [DeclarUI: Bridging Design and Development with Automated Declarative UI Code Generation](https://doi.org/10.1145/3715726) (Proceedings of the ACM on Software Engineering (FSE 2025), 2025) · [code](https://github.com/security-pride/DeclarUI) — Combines visual component extraction, multimodal models, page-transition graphs and iterative checks to reconstruct multi-page mobile UI code from designs or screenshots, evaluated with React Native, Flutter and ArkUI.
- [DecLLM: LLM-Augmented Recompilable Decompilation for Enabling Programmatic Use of Decompiled Code](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3728958) (Proceedings of the ACM on Software Engineering (ISSTA 2025), 2025) — DecLLM repairs decompiler pseudocode that does not recompile into C that compiles and runs, so source-level tools such as CodeQL can be applied to binaries. An iterative loop feeds compiler errors to an off-the-shelf LLM, reverting illegal outputs and large deletions, then uses mismatched test outputs and AddressSanitizer reports from the rebuilt program to repair behavior, and finally fuzzes the original and rebuilt executables for divergences. With GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 the authors report 74% and 78.3% success on 300 Code Contest programs; on Coreutils and CGC binaries, 61.9–70.9% recompile and 48.1–59.0% are fully functional.
- [Decompile-Bench: Million-Scale Binary-Source Function Pairs for Real-World Binary Decompilation](https://papers.nips.cc/paper_files/paper/2025/hash/079cf13ae174c31f148207d94d213bdc-Abstract-Datasets_and_Benchmarks_Track.html) (NeurIPS 2025 Datasets and Benchmarks, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/albertan017/LLM4Decompile) — Introduces two million C/C++ binary-source function pairs using a Compile-Trace-Filter pipeline, with an evaluation suite spanning translated HumanEval/MBPP tasks and recent GitHub functions.
- [DecompileBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating Decompilers in Real-World Scenarios](https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-acl.1194/) (Findings of ACL 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/vul337/DecompileBench) — Evaluates traditional and LLM-based decompilers using functions from real programs, runtime behavioral checks, and an LLM-assisted assessment of analyst-facing code understandability.
- [Decompiling Smart Contracts with a Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.19624) (arXiv, 2025) — Converts Ethereum Virtual Machine bytecode into Solidity in two stages. Static analysis first lifts the bytecode into three-address code with recovered control flow; a Llama-3.2-3B model fine-tuned with LoRA on 238,446 TAC-to-Solidity function pairs then writes each function. On 9,731 held-out functions from verified Ethereum contracts, the authors report average semantic similarity of 0.82 to the original source, with 78.3% of functions above 0.8. An ablation compares the untuned base model on 663 functions, and case studies decompile an unverified Dx Protocol contract and an exploited MEV bot.
- [Design2Code: Benchmarking Multimodal Code Generation for Automated Front-End Engineering](https://aclanthology.org/2025.naacl-long.199/) (NAACL 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/NoviScl/Design2Code) — Evaluates reconstruction of frontend code from webpage screenshots using real-world reference pages, visual-element and layout metrics, and human assessment.
- [Diffusion Models Are Real-Time Game Engines](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2025/hash/b71ecea210f7159f31e46631fe5c838f-Abstract-Conference.html) (ICLR 2025, 2025) — GameNGen learns to simulate DOOM interactively with a diffusion model instead of game code. A PPO agent first plays the game in ViZDoom, and its recorded trajectories, from random to trained play, become the training data. Stable Diffusion v1.4 is then fine-tuned to predict the next frame from the last 64 frames and actions, with noise added to context frames to keep long autoregressive rollouts stable and a fine-tuned latent decoder for detail. With 4 sampling steps it runs at 20 frames per second on one TPU-v5. The authors report next-frame PSNR of 29.43 and human raters picking the real game in 58–60% of short clips.
- [DiSCo: Towards Decompiling EVM Bytecode to Source Code using Large Language Models](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3729373) (Proceedings of the ACM on Software Engineering (FSE 2025), 2025) — DiSCo lifts Ethereum contract creation bytecode to Solidity source by prompting a frozen LLM, instead of emitting decompiler pseudocode. It abstracts control-flow paths into logic-invariant semantic units, recovers constructor logic by simulating creation bytecode captured from block execution, and infers stripped names with signature databases and a type-aware graph model. These units become natural-language descriptions plus coding specifications in the prompt. GPT-4 is the primary model; Llama 3 is also tested.
- [Divide-and-Conquer: Generating UI Code from Screenshots](https://doi.org/10.1145/3729364) (Proceedings of the ACM on Software Engineering (FSE 2025), 2025) · [code](https://github.com/WebPAI/DCGen) — Recursively divides a webpage screenshot along visual separation lines, has a multimodal model generate HTML/CSS for each leaf segment, and assembles child code upward into the full page; evaluated with GPT-4o, Gemini and Claude models and a small developer study.
- [EnIGMA: Interactive Tools Substantially Assist LM Agents in Finding Security Vulnerabilities](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v267/abramovich25a.html) (ICML 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/SWE-agent/SWE-agent/tree/v0.7) — EnIGMA extends SWE-agent for capture-the-flag challenges with interactive agent tools that keep a gdb debugging session or a pwntools server connection open across turns, alongside decompile and disassemble commands, an LM summarizer for long outputs, and category-specific demonstrations and guidelines. It is evaluated with four LMs on 390 challenges from NYU CTF, InterCode-CTF, Cybench and a HackTheBox set the authors collected. The paper ablates tools, summarizers and demonstrations, and analyzes solution leakage and “soliloquizing”, where the model writes its own observations instead of acting in the environment.
- [Exploring the Potential of LLMs for Code Deobfuscation](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-97620-9_15) (DIMVA 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/DavidBeste/llm-code-deobfuscation) — Studies LLMs as source-to-source deobfuscators for C functions protected with the Tigress obfuscator. Functions from ExeBench, with identifiers randomized, are obfuscated with five transformations: MBA arithmetic encoding, branch encoding, control-flow flattening, opaque predicates and argument randomization. Each model receives a whole obfuscated function and returns simplified C. DeepSeek Coder 6.7B and Code Llama 7B are fine-tuned on obfuscated–original pairs, GPT-4 is prompted, and Clang optimization at -O0 to -O3 serves as a compiler baseline. Simplification is scored with Halstead program length relative to the original and obfuscated code, syntactic correctness by compiling ExeBench wrappers and semantic correctness by ExeBench input/output samples. The fine-tuned models reduce complexity more than GPT-4 on every transformation and more than Clang except on arithmetic encoding and argument randomization, where Clang is on par. Trained on chains of up to five transformations, they keep stable scores on chains of six and seven, and the best model cuts Halstead program length by 89.21% on average in the hardest scenario. A test with randomized constants found one sample that reproduced memorized code.
- [ICEPRE: ICS Protocol Reverse Engineering via Data-Driven Concolic Execution](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3728982) (Proceedings of the ACM on Software Engineering (ISSTA 2025), 2025) · [code](https://github.com/QYbPtqyb/ICEPRE) — Infers message-field boundaries by following concrete message-guided paths through binary protocol parsers, then symbolically replaying them to extract byte-level data flow. It merges bytes using value-preserving operations and similar processing traces. For the 1,000-message datasets of four public ICS protocols, the authors report average boundary F1 of 0.76 and field perfection of 0.67.
- [Interaction2Code: Benchmarking MLLM-based Interactive Webpage Code Generation from Interactive Prototyping](https://doi.org/10.1109/ASE63991.2025.00028) (ASE 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/WebPAI/Interaction2Code) — Benchmarks reconstruction of interactive webpages from screenshots before and after an interaction. The final study covers 127 webpages and 374 interactions, comparing rendered appearance, automated interaction checks, and human usability judgments.
- [Is This the Same Code? A Comprehensive Study of Decompilation Techniques for WebAssembly Binaries](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-031-94448-2_6) (SecureComm 2024, 2025) — Measures how well existing WebAssembly decompilers recover C, with native-binary decompilers as the baseline. The same C programs are compiled with Emscripten to WebAssembly and with clang to native code, then decompiled by wasm2c, w2c2 and wasm-decompile or by Ghidra and RetDec. Correctness reuses 1,000 Csmith programs from DecFuzzer, recompiling the output and comparing its execution with the original; code-complexity metrics and AST similarity to the original source are measured on PolyBenchC and CHStone at -O0 to -O2. wasm2c passed all 1,000 correctness programs, against 649 for wasm-decompile, 818 for Ghidra and 775 for RetDec.
- [JsDeObsBench: Measuring and Benchmarking LLMs for JavaScript Deobfuscation](https://doi.org/10.1145/3719027.3744871) (ACM CCS 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/Ch3nYe/JsDeObsBench) — Builds an execution-verifiable JavaScript deobfuscation benchmark by applying seven JavaScript-Obfuscator transformations to Project CodeNet programs with test cases, then scores LLM and tool outputs for syntax, execution, code simplification and similarity to the original code.
- [Lares: LLM-driven Code Slice Semantic Search for Patch Presence Testing](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/11334274) (ASE 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/Siyuan-Li201/Lares) — Lares tests whether a known 1-day vulnerability fix is present in a target binary function without recompiling the project. Its inputs are the target binary, the project source and the patch commit diff. A patch-enhancement step adds data- and control-flow context from the source (via Tree-sitter and Joern) to patches with few changed statements. An LLM then locates the pseudocode slice in the IDA Pro decompilation of the target function that corresponds to each patch slice. Finally, matched slices are normalized into equations and checked for equivalence with the Z3 SMT solver, and the LLM judges the cases Z3 cannot model, deciding whether the function matches the pre-patch or the patched version. On 4,480 test cases from 73 CVEs in OpenSSL, FreeType, tcpdump and libxml2, compiled with GCC 9.4.0 and Clang 6 at O0–O3 for x86, x64 and ARM, the authors report an average F1 of 0.77 on x86 with Claude 3.5 Sonnet, against 0.67 for Robin and 0.68 for PS3, and F1 from 0.71 to 0.77 across five compiler–architecture settings.
- [LaTCoder: Converting Webpage Design to Code with Layout-as-Thought](https://doi.org/10.1145/3711896.3737016) (KDD 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/CGCL-codes/naturalcc/tree/5f84062064947a0e2137ebda0bd3a9ae8ceb1736/examples/latcoder) — LaTCoder converts webpage design images to HTML/CSS by making layout an explicit intermediate step. A rule-based divider recursively finds solid-colored horizontal or vertical lines that avoid OCR-detected text, then merges small regions into grid-aligned blocks. An MLLM writes code for each block with a chain-of-thought prompt. The blocks are then assembled by absolute positioning or by the MLLM, and a reference-free verifier combining MAE and CLIP picks the better result. The paper also introduces CC-HARD, 128 complex-layout pages manually sampled from Common Crawl.
- [Malsight: Exploring Malicious Source Code and Benign Pseudocode for Iterative Binary Malware Summarization](https://doi.org/10.1109/TIFS.2025.3583552) (IEEE Transactions on Information Forensics and Security, 2025) — MALSIGHT writes natural-language summaries of stripped binary malware one function at a time. IDA Pro produces pseudocode and a call graph, and functions are summarized callees first, so each caller receives its callees' summaries as context; a sequence-labeling model also marks key API calls and strings, which drive code-search retrieval and LLM-written annotations. The summarizer, MalT5, is a 0.77B-parameter CodeT5+ model fine-tuned first on MalS, 89,609 malicious C source functions from GitHub with LLM-generated summaries refined by human reviewers, and then on annotated benign pseudocode from the Capybara dataset. The authors also introduce MalP, 500 pseudocode functions for testing, and BLEURT-sum, a fine-tuned BLEURT metric, and report that MalT5 performs comparably to much larger Code Llama models.
- [Nova: Generative Language Models for Assembly Code with Hierarchical Attention and Contrastive Learning](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2025/hash/ef283d62b4bce30854a8d4827f331229-Abstract-Conference.html) (ICLR 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/lt-asset/nova/tree/6dfaf438fb23a8f024d0297653571615631ad8f4) — Nova is a generative LLM for x86-64 assembly, trained from DeepSeek-Coder checkpoints on stripped, normalized gcc -O0 to -O3 assembly built from AnghaBench and The Stack. It adds hierarchical attention that builds per-instruction summaries, and contrastive objectives that keep a function's source and O0–O3 assembly close while ordering them by optimization level. Fine-tuned for decompilation, Nova-1.3B and Nova-6.7B reach 25.17% and 34.36% average Pass@1 on HumanEval-Decompile, 14.84 and 12.41 points above same-size LLM4Decompile; separately fine-tuned models report the highest average Recall@1 for O0-to-O3 binary code similarity on six real-world projects.
- [On Benchmarking Code LLMs for Android Malware Analysis](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3713081.3731745) (ISSTA Companion 2025 (LLMSC Workshop), 2025) — CAMA is a framework for benchmarking how code LLMs read decompiled Android malware. Androguard decompiles each APK into Java functions, and each model returns a structured output per function: a summary, a refined function name and a 0–10 maliciousness score. Two analysis tasks build on these outputs. Malicious-function identification is scored by consistency (whether scores and names stay stable when the model is re-prompted with its own summaries) and by fidelity (how much a LightGBM malware-category classifier loses confidence when the top-scored function descriptors are removed). Malware-purpose summarization is scored by semantic relevance (BLEU, METEOR and ROUGE-L against reference app descriptions). The benchmark holds 118 APKs from 6 categories and 13 families, with 7,542,799 decompiled functions. Of the four models evaluated, StarChat-Beta has the highest maliciousness consistency (0.813, against 0.381 for CodeLlama) and narrowly the highest semantic-relevance scores, while fidelity varies widely across models and removal sizes. Renaming functions with model-suggested names raises fidelity for CodeLlama and StarChat but lowers METEOR by about 10%.
- [PoE-World: Compositional World Modeling with Products of Programmatic Experts](https://papers.nips.cc/paper_files/paper/2025/hash/262dd62fd1bbb30d6a6b4d578f5e65ff-Abstract-Conference.html) (NeurIPS 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/topwasu/poe-world/tree/b499fb16a60793840a8ad97b3739518c7505ca3a) — PoE-World represents an Atari world model as an exponentially weighted product of many small Python programs, or programmatic experts, each synthesized by an LLM to explain one aspect of the observed dynamics; learned weights and optional synthesized hard constraints combine them. From object lists that OCAtari parses out of a demonstration of fewer than 1,000 frames, the authors learn stochastic, partially observed models of Pong and Montezuma's Revenge and use them in a planning agent, including on rearranged Pong-Alt and Montezuma's Revenge-Alt variants that have no demonstrations.
- [ReF Decompile: Relabeling and Function Call Enhanced Decompile](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.12221) (arXiv, 2025) — ReF Decompile relabels jump targets and uses function calls to recover variable information when converting binary code to source code. It was evaluated on the HumanEval-Decompile Benchmark.
- [REx86: A Local Large Language Model for Assisting in x86 Assembly Reverse Engineering](https://doi.org/10.1109/ACSAC67867.2025.00024) (2025 IEEE Annual Computer Security Applications Conference (ACSAC), 2025) · [code](https://github.com/dlea8/REx86) — The paper fine-tunes eight local models from CodeLlama, Qwen2.5-Coder, and CodeGemma on 5,981 x86 assembly examples and evaluates their help with binary reverse engineering.
- [Sketch2Code: Evaluating Vision-Language Models for Interactive Web Design Prototyping](https://aclanthology.org/2025.naacl-long.198/) (NAACL 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/SALT-NLP/Sketch2Code) — Sketch2Code benchmarks vision-language models on turning rough hand-drawn wireframe sketches into HTML webpage prototypes. Crowd annotators drew 731 sketches of 484 real-world webpages from the Design2Code test set. Ten models, eight commercial and two 8B open-source, generate pages directly and in two multi-turn settings with a GPT-4o simulated user: following its feedback, or asking it clarification questions. Outputs are scored by Design2Code visual similarity and a new IoU-based layout similarity over component types such as text blocks, images, navigation bars, forms and buttons. Claude 3.5 Sonnet and GPT-4o lead single-turn layout similarity, and feedback rounds help commercial models more than question asking does.
- [StructEval: Benchmarking LLMs' Capabilities to Generate Structural Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.20139) (arXiv, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/TIGER-AI-Lab/StructEval) — Evaluates generation and conversion of structured outputs, including HTML, React and SVG, with separate checks for format adherence and structural correctness.
