- [Target-Centric Firmware Rehosting with Penguin](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/auto-draft-634/) (Workshop on Binary Analysis Research (BAR) 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/rehosting/penguin) — Penguin rehosts embedded Linux firmware from an extracted filesystem by deriving an editable configuration of emulation transformations, such as file changes, pseudofiles, NVRAM values and network interfaces, from static analysis. Dynamic-analysis plugins expose failures so the configuration can be refined for each target, and a minimizer identifies which transformations a working rehosting needs.
- [TypeForge: Synthesizing and Selecting Best-Fit Composite Data Types for Stripped Binaries](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/11023499) (IEEE S&P 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/noobone123/TypeForge) — TypeForge recovers composite data types, including struct layouts, unions and pointer or nesting relationships, from stripped binaries. A whole-program Type Flow Graph over Ghidra’s decompiler-inferred variables propagates member offsets and sizes, cuts conflicting edges caused by casts and unions, and synthesizes candidate type declarations. An LLM only selects among these statically synthesized candidates: each is applied through the decompiler’s retype interface, and a double-elimination tournament compares the resulting pseudocode for readability. On OSPREY’s coreutils dataset the authors report F1 of 81.7% for composite-type identification, 88.2% for layout recovery and 72.1% for relationship recovery.
- [UIOrchestra: Generating High-Fidelity Code from UI Designs with a Multi-agent System](https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-emnlp.150/) (Findings of EMNLP 2025, 2025) — UIOrchestra converts static mobile UI designs into React/CSS through layout-description, code-generation and rendered-image comparison agents. Its APPUI benchmark contains 1,101 human-refined code/rendering pairs based on screenshots from 165 apps across 12 categories. The reference code is reconstructed for evaluation, rather than recovered original application source.
- [Unleashing the Power of Generative Model in Recovering Variable Names from Stripped Binary](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/unleashing-the-power-of-generative-model-in-recovering-variable-names-from-stripped-binary/) (NDSS 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/XZ-X/gennm-ndss-ae/tree/9955139b4c8d43144852a674bf317cbfaf32ed17) — GenNm suggests names for placeholder variables in decompiled code of stripped binaries by fine-tuning generative code models (CodeGemma-2B by default; CodeLlama-7B and a LoRA-tuned CodeLlama-34B are also tested). Queries add renamed caller code and callee signatures propagated along the call graph; a DPO-style symbol-preference optimization (SymPO) counters bias toward frequent training names, and a data-flow name-validation step chooses among candidates over iterative rounds. The authors report 5.6–11.4-point precision gains over VarBERT and ReSym on rebuilt DIRTY and VarCorpus datasets, and 22.8% precision on names unseen in training against 17.3% for the strongest baseline.
- [Unleashing the Power of LLM to Infer State Machine From the Protocol Implementation](https://doi.org/10.1109/IWQoS65803.2025.11143461) (IWQoS 2025, 2025) — ProtocolGPT infers protocol state machines from implementation source code with a retrieval-augmented LLM. It extracts state-machine-related code with regular expressions and a code filter, splits it into syntax-aware chunks stored in a FAISS vector store with OpenAI embeddings, and prompts GPT-4 step by step to recover states and message-triggered transitions. On six protocols in five open-source code bases (IKEv2, TLS 1.3, TLS 1.2, BGP, RTSP and L2TP), the authors report transition precision above 90%, more than 30% higher precision and 55% higher recall than baselines. Seeding AFLNet with the inferred machines raised coverage by more than 20% and found two previously unknown crash bugs in an RTSP server.
- [WAFFLE: Fine-tuning Multi-Modal Model for Automated Front-End Development](https://aclanthology.org/2025.acl-long.1208/) (ACL 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/lt-asset/Waffle/tree/5ca57f8b64b0fa8827f1f310afe6ac1afc63d088) — WAFFLE fine-tunes multimodal LLMs to generate HTML/CSS from UI screenshots. A quarter of the decoder's attention heads use a structure-aware mask that limits each element's tokens to its parent, preceding siblings and its own earlier tokens. A contrastive objective aligns image and code embeddings over groups of four mutated WebSight-v0.1 pages. With VLM-WebSight and Moondream2 backbones, the authors report up to 9.00 pp higher HTML-Match and 0.0982 higher CW-SSIM than standard fine-tuning on WebSight-Test, plus gains on every Design2Code metric.
- [WebCode2M: A Real-World Dataset for Code Generation from Webpage Designs](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3696410.3714889) (WWW 2025, 2025) · [code](https://github.com/CGCL-codes/naturalcc/tree/main/examples/webcode2m) — WebCode2M pairs about 2.56 million real-world webpage screenshots with purified HTML/CSS code and element bounding-box layout trees. Pages sampled from a Common Crawl snapshot are cleaned (scripts, metadata and hidden elements removed, styles merged into one block), rendered with Playwright, and filtered by a neural scorer trained on 10,000 annotated samples. The paper fine-tunes Pix2Struct-1.3B as the WebCoder baseline, introduces TreeBLEU to measure DOM-structure recall, and benchmarks specialized, open and commercial multimodal models on short, medium and long test subsets of 256 pages each.
- [WebUIBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating Multimodal Large Language Models in WebUI-to-Code](https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-acl.815/) (Findings of ACL 2025, 2025) — Diagnoses webpage-to-code capability through visual perception, HTML programming, image-to-HTML understanding, and screenshot reconstruction tasks. It separates underlying skills using question-answer pairs derived from real websites.
- [A Taxonomy of C Decompiler Fidelity Issues](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity24/presentation/dramko) (USENIX Security 2024, 2024) — Open-codes decompiled/original C function pairs to classify how decompiler output departs from the source beyond missing names and types. Using a statement-alignment abstraction and thematic analysis, the authors build a hierarchical codebook of 15 top-level categories and 52 codes, covering correctness issues such as nonequivalent expressions and incorrect return behavior and readability issues such as extraneous variables, non-idiomatic control flow and decompiler-specific macros. The codebook, developed on Hex-Rays output, is applied to Ghidra, retdec and angr, and the authors suggest type prediction, rule-based fixes and whole-program learned techniques to address classes of defects.
- [Assemblage: Automatic Binary Dataset Construction for Machine Learning](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/6bbefc73a187dd42e0dc065b4e7a0615-Abstract-Datasets_and_Benchmarks_Track.html) (NeurIPS 2024 Datasets and Benchmarks, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/Assemblage-Dataset/Assemblage) — Assemblage is a distributed system that crawls GitHub and vcpkg, builds C/C++ projects under many compiler configurations, and records source-to-binary mappings such as function addresses, source code, comments and PDB or DWARF debug information. Run on AWS for about a year, it produced 890k Windows PE binaries built with Visual Studio 2015–2022 across optimization levels, architectures and Debug/Release modes, plus 428k Linux ELF binaries from GCC and Clang, in 29 configurations. Case studies use the data for compiler provenance with PassTell, function similarity with a graph neural network and jTrans, and function-boundary identification comparing XDA with IDA.
- [Assessing LLMs in Malicious Code Deobfuscation of Real-world Malware Campaigns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.19715) (arXiv, 2024) — The paper evaluates four LLMs on deobfuscating real-world malicious scripts from the Emotet malware campaign and examines their use in reverse engineering.
- [BinaryAI: Binary Software Composition Analysis via Intelligent Binary Source Code Matching](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3597503.3639100) (ICSE 2024, 2024) — BinaryAI identifies which open-source C/C++ libraries a stripped binary reuses by matching its functions to source code. Ghidra decompiles each binary function to pseudo-C, and a Pythia-410M model, trained with a CLIP-style contrastive loss on about 10 million binary–source function pairs, embeds pseudo-code and source functions in one space. Candidates retrieved from a corpus of 56,342,179 source functions from 12,013 libraries are refined with link-time locality and the call graph, and a library is reported when the share of its matched functions exceeds a threshold. The authors report 22.54% recall@1 against 10.75% for CodeCMR, and library-detection precision and recall of 85.84% and 64.98% against 73.36% and 59.81% for an unnamed commercial tool.
- [BinPRE: Enhancing Field Inference in Binary Analysis Based Protocol Reverse Engineering](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3658644.3690299) (ACM CCS 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/ecnusse/BinPRE) — Infers protocol-message field boundaries and semantics from instrumented server binaries and input messages. BinPRE groups bytes by the instruction operators that process them, applies detectors for field types and functions, then refines results across similar messages. On eight protocols with 50 messages each, the authors report average field perfection of 0.73 and type/function F1 scores of 0.74/0.81.
- [CEBin: A Cost-Effective Framework for Large-Scale Binary Code Similarity Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.18818) (arXiv, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/Hustcw/CEBin) — CEBin combines code embeddings to shortlist candidate functions with pairwise comparison to detect similar binary code, including vulnerable functions.
- [CLAP: Learning Transferable Binary Code Representations with Natural Language Supervision](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3650212.3652145) (ACM ISSTA 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/Hustcw/CLAP) — CLAP (Contrastive Language-Assembly Pre-training) learns assembly-function embeddings aligned with natural-language explanations. A dataset engine compiles Ubuntu C/C++ packages with GCC and Clang at O0–O3 and Os, has GPT-3.5 explain about 2.6 million matching source functions, and fine-tunes a Vicuna-13B “shadow model” on half of those explanations to label more code, yielding 195 million assembly–explanation pairs. A 110M-parameter assembly encoder, using address rebasing, a WordPiece tokenizer and jump-aware embeddings, is first pre-trained on assembly and then contrastively aligned with a text encoder. The embeddings support zero-shot classification by matching code against text prompts. On BinaryCorp-3M similarity search in pools of 10,000 functions, zero-shot CLAP reports 0.836 average recall@1 against 0.571 for fine-tuned jTrans.
- [Code is not Natural Language: Unlock the Power of Semantics-Oriented Graph Representation for Binary Code Similarity Detection](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity24/presentation/he-haojie) (USENIX Security 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/NSSL-SJTU/HermesSim) — HermesSim detects binary code similarity by lifting each function to Ghidra P-code and building a semantics-oriented graph that exposes operand structure inside instructions, def-use, control-flow and effect-order relations, while dropping semantics-independent details such as which register temporarily holds a value. A gated graph neural network with a multi-head softmax aggregator embeds the graph, and functions are compared by embedding similarity. On the Dataset-1 benchmark of Marcelli et al., the authors report 48.1% RECALL@1 for x64 cross-compiler retrieval in pools of 10,000 functions, against 31.4% for a fine-tuned jTrans, with a 388K-parameter model.
- [CodeArt: Better Code Models by Attention Regularization When Symbols Are Lacking](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3643752) (Proceedings of the ACM on Software Engineering (FSE 2024), 2024) · [code](https://github.com/ziansu/codeart) — CodeArt pre-trains a BERT-sized encoder from scratch on 26 million stripped x86-64 functions built from jTrans's BinaryCorp. Instead of relying on symbols, it uses IDA Pro control-flow graphs and a binary data-flow analysis to compute control and data dependences, then restricts self-attention with masks so a token attends only to instructions it transitively depends on or that depend on it, to tokens in its own instruction, and to a global [CLS] token; pre-training masks tokens and perturbs the dependence graph. The authors fine-tune it for binary similarity, malware family classification and type inference. They report improvements over prior models from 53% to 64% recall@1 for similarity in pools of 500, from 49% to 60% LRAP for malware families, and from 74% to 94% average F1 for type inference.
- [Cross-Inlining Binary Function Similarity Detection](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3597503.3639080) (ICSE 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/island255/cross-inlining_binary_function_similarity) — Studies binary function similarity when function inlining makes one binary function correspond to several source functions. From BinKit builds of 51 GNU projects with and without -fno-inline (9 GCC and Clang versions, O0–O3, six x86, ARM and MIPS targets; 216 combinations each), debug-line mappings label 5,621,140 cross-inlining pairs. The authors identify Leaf-, Root- and Internal-Inlining patterns, show that Gemini, SAFE and GMN lose accuracy on them, and propose CI-Detector, which trains one attributed-CFG graph neural network per pattern and aggregates their scores, reporting 81% precision and 97% recall.
- [D-Helix: A Generic Decompiler Testing Framework Using Symbolic Differentiation](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity24/presentation/zou) (USENIX Security 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/purseclab/D-helix) — Tests decompilers function by function on binaries without needing source code. RECOMPILER repairs decompiled C so individual functions recompile, SYMDIFF compares symbolic models of the original binary function and the recompiled decompiled function using patched angr and PROMPT symbolic executors, and TUNER toggles decompiler heuristic rules to locate, and sometimes work around, the rule behind an inaccuracy. On Ghidra 10.0 and angr 9.2.30, 2,004 binaries and object files from coreutils, util-linux and six popular GitHub C projects yield 93K decompiled functions; the authors report 4,515 incorrectly decompiled functions, 8 reproduced known bugs, 17 previously unknown bugs and 7 bugs fixed automatically.
- [DeGPT: Optimizing Decompiler Output with LLM](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/degpt-optimizing-decompiler-output-with-llm/) (NDSS 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/PeiweiHu/DeGPT) — Refines existing C-like decompiler output through three coordinated roles that select, propose and check changes. It simplifies structures, adds comments and renames variables; Micro Snippet Semantic Calculation compares simulated symbol values and calls before accepting structural changes. The authors evaluate readability and naming on 620 functions from four codebases, with separate stripped and non-stripped variants.
- [DynPRE: Protocol Reverse Engineering via Dynamic Inference](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/dynpre-protocol-reverse-engineering-via-dynamic-inference/) (NDSS 2024, 2024) — DynPRE starts from captured client–server traces and then interacts with a live protocol server to infer message formats and types. It detects session-specific identifiers such as cookies and rewrites them on the fly so that replayed requests stay valid. It then sends byte-level probe requests whose responses serve as semantic feedback for field identification, aligns the responses, and applies a request–response mapping refinement that also recognizes message types. On 12 protocols with 10-, 100- and 1,000-message datasets, the authors report average field perfection of 0.50 and message-type V-measure of 0.94, against 0.15–0.31 perfection for Netzob, NetPlier, FieldHunter, BinaryInferno and NEMESYS.
- [Exploring the Efficacy of Large Language Models (GPT-4) in Binary Reverse Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.06637) (arXiv, 2024) — The paper evaluates GPT-4 for binary reverse engineering through interpretation of human-written and decompiled code, from basic code explanation to malware analysis.
- [FoC: Figure out the Cryptographic Functions in Stripped Binaries with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.18403) (arXiv, 2024) — FoC uses FoC-BinLLM to summarize cryptographic functions in stripped binaries and FoC-Sim to retrieve similar implementations. It evaluates both models and applications to virus analysis and 1-day vulnerability detection.
- [Generating Code World Models with Large Language Models Guided by Monte Carlo Tree Search](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/6f479ea488e0908ac8b1b37b27fd134c-Abstract-Conference.html) (NeurIPS 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/nicoladainese96/code-world-models) — GIF-MCTS generates executable Python transition and reward models from natural-language environment descriptions and offline state-transition trajectories, using prediction feedback to generate, improve and repair code. CWMB evaluates 18 reinforcement-learning environments and downstream planning; a separate experiment uses a simplified Read to Fight Monsters grid world.
- [How Far Have We Gone in Binary Code Understanding Using Large Language Models](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/10795058) (ICSME 2024, 2024) — Benchmarks 16 code and general LLMs on function name recovery and summarization of stripped binaries. The benchmark takes 12 popular C projects built for x86-64, strips and decompiles 28 binaries with IDA Pro, aligns 81,185 functions with source through DWARF, and keeps 2,000 functions after expert review. Zero-shot LLMs are compared with SymLM, NER, BinT5 and HexT5. Code Llama 34B leads name recovery with token-level F1 of 27.59, and ChatGPT leads summarization with BLEU-4 7.37. The study also measures few-shot prompting, LoRA fine-tuning of two 7B models on GNU binaries, and a case study on a stripped open-source Linux remote-access trojan.
- [Large Language Models for Code Analysis: Do LLMs Really Do Their Job?](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity24/presentation/fang) (USENIX Security 2024, 2024) — Evaluates GPT-3.5-turbo, GPT-4, LLaMA-2-13B, Code-LLaMA-2-13B-Instruct and StarChat-Beta on explaining what de-commented JavaScript, Python and C code does, then repeats the task on JavaScript obfuscated with JavaScript Obfuscator (identifier renaming, dead-code injection, control-flow flattening and string splitting) and with Wobfuscator’s WebAssembly transformation. GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 are also asked to rewrite 100 IOCCC winning programs as readable, compilable code. Case studies apply GPT models to a botnet and a benign browser-extension repository, an Android SMS stealer decompiled with JD-GUI, and WannaCry code decompiled with RetDec.
- [LLM4Decompile: Decompiling Binary Code with Large Language Models](https://aclanthology.org/2024.emnlp-main.203/) (EMNLP 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/albertan017/LLM4Decompile) — Trains language models for assembly-to-C decompilation and for refining Ghidra pseudocode, evaluating whether recovered functions can be recompiled and executed correctly.
- [NYU CTF Bench: A Scalable Open-Source Benchmark Dataset for Evaluating LLMs in Offensive Security](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/69d97a6493fbf016fff0a751f253ad18-Abstract-Datasets_and_Benchmarks_Track.html) (NeurIPS 2024 Datasets and Benchmarks, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/NYU-LLM-CTF/NYU_CTF_Bench) — NYU CTF Bench packages 200 manually validated challenges from CSAW CTF qualifying and final rounds held between 2017 and 2023, spanning cryptography, forensics, pwn, reverse engineering, web and miscellaneous tasks, with challenge.json metadata and Docker images for server-side challenges. The paper also presents an automated framework that gives LLMs function-calling tools for shell commands, file creation, Ghidra disassembly and decompilation, flag checking and giving up. It evaluates five LLMs and compares their scores with human teams at CSAW 2022 and 2023.
- [ReSym: Harnessing LLMs to Recover Variable and Data Structure Symbols from Stripped Binaries](https://doi.org/10.1145/3658644.3670340) (ACM CCS 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/lt-asset/resym) — Combines two fine-tuned language models with Prolog-based cross-function reasoning to infer local-variable symbols and user-defined data structures from stripped-binary decompilation.
- [Self-Constructed Context Decompilation with Fined-grained Alignment Enhancement](https://aclanthology.org/2024.findings-emnlp.385/) (Findings of EMNLP 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/AlongWY/sccdec) — Adds two techniques to LLM decompilation of function-level x86-64 GCC assembly into C. Self-Constructed Context Decompilation (sc²dec) recompiles a model’s first compilable output and uses the resulting assembly–source pair as an in-context example for a second attempt, without fine-tuning. Fine-grained Alignment Enhancement (FAE) fine-tunes llm4decompile-6.7b with LoRA on statement-level assembly–C alignments derived from DWARF debug information. Combining both, the authors report 52.41% average re-executability on Decompile-Eval, against 48.60% for the base model.
- [SLaDe: A Portable Small Language Model Decompiler for Optimized Assembly](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/10444788) (IEEE/ACM CGO 2024, 2024) · [code](https://zenodo.org/records/10205121) — SLaDe translates function-level assembly into C with a 200M-parameter encoder–decoder model, code-specific tokenization and PsycheC type completion. It studies x86 and ARM at -O0 and -O3, combining generated candidates with input/output checks. The input is assembly text, not raw machine bytes; disassembly is outside the method.
- [StackSight: Unveiling WebAssembly through Large Language Models and Neurosymbolic Chain-of-Thought Decompilation](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v235/fang24e.html) (ICML 2024, 2024) — StackSight decompiles WebAssembly text-format functions into C++ with LLM prompting. A static analysis annotates each WAT instruction with the virtual-stack state, using symbolic notation where branches diverge; a one-shot chain-of-thought prompt then asks the model to recover variable meanings, summarize the function and write C++. With GPT-4 the share of outputs passing all benchmark tests rises from 19.7% to 36.5% on HumanEval-X and from 42.4% to 59.6% on MBXP relative to one-shot prompting; GPT-3.5 and Code Llama-7B-Instruct are also evaluated.
- [STRIDE: Simple Type Recognition In Decompiled Executables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.02733) (arXiv, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/hgarrereyn/STRIDE) — STRIDE predicts the names and types of variables in decompiler output without a neural model. From training decompilation with known labels, it builds a database mapping the token sequences (N-grams, with N from 2 to 60) immediately left and right of each variable occurrence to the five most frequent names or types. Variable placeholders are canonicalized, and address-like tokens and large constants are bucketed so that N-grams can match. At prediction time it looks up the longest matching N-gram on each side of every occurrence, scores the returned labels by how specific the match is, and sums the scores across the function. The prototype is about 1,000 lines of Python and runs on a CPU. On DIRT functions not seen in training, the authors report 66.4% retyping accuracy, against 52.3% for DIRTY with beam search, and 56.2% renaming accuracy, against 51.3% reported for VarBERT. Across 16 VarCorpus splits, renaming accuracy is higher than VarBERT on 11 and 1.55 points higher on average. Prediction takes 8.2 ms per function on a CPU, against 43.7 ms for VarBERT on an NVIDIA L4 GPU.
- [TYGR: Type Inference on Stripped Binaries using Graph Neural Networks](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity24/presentation/zhu-chang) (USENIX Security 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/sefcom/TYGR) — TYGR infers the types of stack and heap variables in stripped binaries by running an architecture-agnostic, intra-procedural data-flow analysis on angr's VEX IR and classifying each variable's data-flow graph with a graph neural network. It predicts base types, pointers and struct member layouts, covering 97.1% of the types observed in its data. The authors also build TyDa, 163,643 binaries compiled with debug information from Gentoo and Debian C packages for x86, x64, AArch64, Arm32 and MIPS at O0–O3, and deduplicate functions in the evaluated subset before splitting it. On x64 across O0–O3 they report 76.6% overall and 45.2% struct accuracy, and say TYGR beats prior work by at least 26.1% overall and 10.2% on structs.
- [Unlocking the conversion of Web Screenshots into HTML Code with the WebSight Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.09029) (arXiv, 2024) — WebSight is a synthetic dataset of 2 million HTML documents paired with full-page screenshots for training screenshot-to-HTML models. Mistral-7B-Instruct generates website concepts, Deepseek-Coder-33B-Instruct writes each page in HTML with Tailwind CSS, images come from keyword-based stock-photo URLs, and Playwright renders the screenshots after filtering. This v0.2 release replaces the 823K-pair v0.1, which used traditional CSS and colored rectangles instead of images. The authors fine-tune an 8B vision-language model, Sightseer, on the data with DoRA and show qualitative conversions, including one from a hand-drawn sketch.
- [WaDec: Decompiling WebAssembly Using Large Language Model](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3691620.3695020) (ASE 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/XinyuShe/WaDec/tree/e244f7172539478a5b64bbb86e1fd03e527cb6be) — WaDec decompiles WebAssembly text (wat) into C by fine-tuning CodeLlama-7b with LoRA on loop-level wat–C snippet pairs. Functions are ordered by dependency and sliced so that each input holds at most one loop, with nested loops replaced by markers. Prompts add declarations of called functions, previously defined local variables and offset-to-string mappings; the outputs are then reassembled, with string constants restored and variables renamed. Training data come from 51,768 the-stack-dedup and 377 TheAlgorithms/C programs compiled with Emscripten. On 3,977 C files, the authors report 3.34% code inflation, 52.11% recompilability, 43.55% re-execution and 27.15% output consistency, compared with Wasm2c and Ghidra.
- [Web2Code: A Large-scale Webpage-to-Code Dataset and Evaluation Framework for Multimodal LLMs](https://proceedings.nips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/cb66be286795d71f89367d596bf78ea7-Abstract-Datasets_and_Benchmarks_Track.html) (NeurIPS 2024 Datasets and Benchmarks, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/MBZUAI-LLM/web2code) — Studies screenshot-to-HTML reconstruction through instruction tuning and two evaluation benchmarks. Web2Code combines generated webpages, refined WebSight and pix2code image/code pairs, and WebSRC-derived question answering. Models produce HTML from a webpage image and instruction; rendered output is compared visually, while a separate benchmark measures screenshot understanding.
- [WorldCoder, a Model-Based LLM Agent: Building World Models by Writing Code and Interacting with the Environment](https://papers.nips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/820c61a0cd419163ccbd2c33b268816e-Abstract-Conference.html) (NeurIPS 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/haotang1995/WorldCoder) — WorldCoder learns executable Python transition and reward models from environment interactions and natural-language goals. It refines code against recorded experiences and an optimistic planning constraint, studying transfer and task solving in Sokoban, MiniGrid and ALFWorld.
- [“Len or index or count, anything but v1”: Predicting Variable Names in Decompilation Output with Transfer Learning](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/10646727) (IEEE S&P 2024, 2024) · [code](https://github.com/sefcom/VarBERT/tree/76f221a5e0af43a36169818cbbfd6e8dd3c98cdc) — VarBERT suggests variable names for IDA Hex-Rays and Ghidra decompiler output with a BERT-style model pre-trained on 5.2M human-written C functions from Debian and fine-tuned on decompiled code; it also predicts whether each variable came from the developer or was introduced by the decompiler. The authors build VarCorpus from Gentoo C and C++ packages compiled for x86-64 at O0–O3, labeling decompiled variables from debug symbols. On per-function splits of O2 binaries, top-1 accuracy is 54.43% for IDA and 54.49% for Ghidra; on an IDA-O0 subset it reaches 50.70%, against 35.94% for DIRE and 38.00% for DIRTY.
- [Asteria-Pro: Enhancing Deep-Learning Based Binary Code Similarity Detection by Incorporating Domain Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2301.00511) (arXiv, 2023) — Asteria-Pro adds domain-knowledge pre-filtration and re-ranking to Tree-LSTM-based binary code similarity detection. It evaluates firmware vulnerability search across diverse IoT hardware architectures.
- [Binary Code Summarization: Benchmarking ChatGPT/GPT-4 and Other Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.09601) (arXiv, 2023) — BinSum benchmarks LLMs on summarizing binary functions in natural language. The authors compile 44 GNU projects with GCC 7.5 for x86, x64, ARM and MIPS at -O0 to -O3, match 557,664 binary functions to developer comments as reference summaries, and present each function as raw bytes, assembly, IDA microcode or decompiled code with and without symbols. GPT-4, ChatGPT, Llama 2 and Code Llama receive prompts selected from 320 GPT-4-generated candidates, with the fine-tuned BinT5 as a baseline; outputs are scored by embedding similarity alongside BLEU, METEOR and ROUGE-L. Decompiled code is the most useful representation, and stripping symbols lowers the average similarity score from 0.449 to 0.202.
- [BinaryInferno: A Semantic-Driven Approach to Field Inference for Binary Message Formats](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/binaryinferno-a-semantic-driven-approach-to-field-inference-for-binary-message-formats/) (NDSS 2023, 2023) · [code](https://github.com/binaryinferno/binaryinferno) — Infers partial binary-message formats from static message samples without instrumenting an executable. Specialized detectors recognize floats, timestamps, lengths, entropy boundaries and serialization patterns; a graph-based integration step reconciles their descriptions. Across samples from ten protocols, the authors report top-level field-boundary precision of 0.69, recall of 0.73 and false-positive rate of 0.04 for the 1,000-message setting without analyst priors.
- [Combining Functional and Automata Synthesis to Discover Causal Reactive Programs](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3571249) (Proceedings of the ACM on Programming Languages (POPL 2023), 2023) — AutumnSynth synthesizes a program in Autumn, a functional reactive language for Atari-style grid worlds, from a trace of observed frames and user actions such as clicks and arrow keys. Heuristic perception parses each frame into typed objects and tracking links them across time steps. The synthesizer then finds an update function for each observed change and searches for the event that triggers it. When no event over the observed state explains an update, automata synthesis invents latent state, such as counters or modes, using a SAT-based Sketch encoding or a faster heuristic. The authors introduce the 30-program Causal Inductive Synthesis Corpus (CISC), on which the heuristic variant synthesizes 27 programs. A slightly modified version synthesizes 21 of 27 games from the third-party EMPA grid-world corpus.
- [Decompiling x86 Deep Neural Network Executables](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity23/presentation/liu-zhibo) (USENIX Security 2023, 2023) · [code](https://github.com/monkbai/DNN-decompiler/tree/33227a0b34f87c490efc734d667ac56530f5074a) — BTD (Bin to DNN) decompiles stripped 64-bit x86 executables emitted by deep-learning compilers into model specifications: operator types, network topology, dimensions and parameters. A learned classifier labels disassembled functions as DNN operators, Intel Pin dynamic analysis chains them, and taint-reduced, trace-based symbolic execution with per-operator patterns recovers dimensions and parameter layouts. The authors rebuild recovered models in PyTorch and recompile them to compare behaviour with the original executable.
- [Extracting Protocol Format as State Machine via Controlled Static Loop Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.13483) (arXiv, 2023) — The paper extracts protocol message formats from parser code by analyzing parsing loops as state machines. It evaluates format inference and the use of the extracted machines in protocol fuzzers.
- [kTrans: Knowledge-Aware Transformer for Binary Code Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.12659) (arXiv, 2023) · [code](https://github.com/Learner0x5a/kTrans-release) — kTrans uses a knowledge-aware Transformer to embed binary code. It evaluates the embeddings through outlier detection and visualization, and on binary code similarity, function type recovery, and indirect call recognition.
- [Lifting Network Protocol Implementation to Precise Format Specification with Security Applications](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3576915.3616614) (ACM CCS 2023, 2023) · [code](https://github.com/qingkaishi/netlifter) — NetLifter statically lifts source-available C protocol parsers into BNF-style message grammars and first-order constraints between packet fields. It analyzes annotated LLVM bitcode using abstract format graphs, localized path-sensitive analysis, unfolding and reordering. The resulting field boundaries, constraints and names can support packet generation and parser construction.
- [Refining Decompiled C Code with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.06530) (arXiv, 2023) — The paper proposes a two-step hybrid LLM approach to refine IDA-Pro decompiled C code for recompilation. On popular C test cases, it reports a recompilation success rate above 75% with moderate effort.
- [Revisiting Binary Code Similarity Analysis Using Interpretable Feature Engineering and Lessons Learned](https://doi.org/10.1109/TSE.2022.3187689) (IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering, 2023) · [code](https://github.com/SoftSec-KAIST/BinKit) — Builds BinKit, a reproducible cross-compilation benchmark of 243,128 binaries and 36,256,322 functions from 51 GNU packages, covering 1,352 combinations of eight architectures, nine GCC and Clang versions, five optimization levels, and PIE, LTO, no-inline and Obfuscator-LLVM options. TikNib, an interpretable baseline over simple presemantic function features, measures how compilers, options and architectures change those features. The authors report that a few basic features approach a deep-learning tool’s accuracy, that type features push AUC above 0.99, and trace failures to IDA Pro analysis errors, compiler back-end differences and architecture-specific code.
- [XFL: Naming Functions in Binaries with Extreme Multi-label Learning](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/10179439) (IEEE S&P 2023, 2023) · [code](https://github.com/lmu-plai/xfl) — XFL treats function naming in stripped binaries as extreme multi-label classification: names are split into tokens, and PfastreXML ranks which tokens from a vocabulary of frequent labels fit each function. Its DEXTER embedding combines static-analysis features built on angr’s VEX IR with call-graph and whole-binary context, and a separate language model orders predicted tokens into a name string. On 741,724 functions from 10,047 x86-64 C binaries in Debian packages, with a 1,024-label vocabulary, the authors report precision 0.835, recall 0.575 and F1 0.681, ahead of Punstrip, Debin and Nero in precision and F1.
- [Augmenting Decompiler Output with Learned Variable Names and Types](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity22/presentation/chen-qibin) (USENIX Security 2022, 2022) · [code](https://github.com/CMUSTRUDEL/DIRTY) — Introduces DIRTY, a Transformer model that recommends variable names and types from decompiled code and layout information, together with the DIRT corpus for training and evaluation.
- [Automated Attack Synthesis by Extracting Finite State Machines from Protocol Specification Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.09470) (IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy 2022, 2022) — Extracts protocol FSMs from RFC prose using technical word representations, zero-shot text mapping, and rules, then uses the extracted FSMs to synthesize attacks.
- [Beyond the C: Retargetable Decompilation using Neural Machine Translation](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/auto-draft-298/) (Workshop on Binary Analysis Research (BAR) 2022, 2022) · [code](https://figshare.com/s/2c68b9c181e80f4e3b06) — BTC (Beyond The C) treats decompilation as plain-text translation from compiler-emitted x86-64 assembly to source code. It uses byte-pair-encoding tokenizers trained separately on assembly and source, and a standard fairseq transformer. Function pairs are matched through the compiler's CFI and line directives rather than language-specific parsers, compiler plugins or lifters, so the same pipeline is retrained for Go, Fortran, OCaml and C. Training sets range from about 10,000 functions for small C, Go and Fortran corpora to 2 million C functions built from Debian packages. The best model, trained on the large C set, reaches a normalized average edit distance of 0.46 on held-out functions.
- [Decomperson: How Humans Decompile and What We Can Learn From It](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity22/presentation/burk) (USENIX Security 2022, 2022) · [code](https://github.com/decompetition/server) — Reframes manual reverse engineering as perfect decompilation: writing source that, compiled with the original toolchain and options, reproduces the target functions’ binary code exactly. The authors built Decomperson, a web editor that compiles, disassembles, tests and diffs each submission, and used it in Decompetition, a 24-hour Jeopardy-style competition in November 2020 with 23 unstripped challenge binaries written in C, C++, Go, Rust and Swift. Over 150 participants made more than 35,000 submissions, including over 300 perfect ones; all but one challenge was perfectly decompiled, and the recorded edit histories are analyzed to describe how humans decompile.
- [DeepDi: Learning a Relational Graph Convolutional Network Model on Instructions for Fast and Accurate Disassembly](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity22/presentation/yu-sheng) (USENIX Security 2022, 2022) · [code](https://github.com/DeepBitsTechnology/DeepDi) — DeepDi disassembles x86 and x64 binaries by decoding a superset of candidate instructions, linking them in an Instruction Flow Graph of forward, backward and overlap relations, and classifying true instructions with a Relational Graph Convolutional Network; heuristics and a small classifier then recover function entry points. On BAP corpora, LLVM 11 built with MSVC on Windows, and SPEC CPU2006 and CPU2017, the authors report accuracy comparable or superior to IDA Pro, Binary Ninja, Ghidra, Datalog Disassembly and XDA, robustness on Linn–Debray and Hikari obfuscated binaries, single-core CPU throughput of 146 KB/s (about twice IDA Pro) and GPU throughput of 24.5 MB/s (about 350 times IDA Pro).
- [Elipmoc: Advanced Decompilation of Ethereum Smart Contracts](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3527321) (Proceedings of the ACM on Programming Languages (OOPSLA 2022), 2022) · [code](https://github.com/nevillegrech/gigahorse-toolchain) — Elipmoc decompiles Ethereum Virtual Machine bytecode into a structured three-address intermediate representation for security analyses and reverse engineering. Built as an evolution of the Datalog-based Gigahorse decompiler, it adds transactional context sensitivity, path-sensitive inference of function arguments and returns with path merging, and fully context-sensitive reconstruction of private functions. On a uniform random sample of 5,000 unique mainnet contracts first deployed between April and September 2021, the authors report fully resolved operands for 99.5% of contracts versus 62.8% for Gigahorse, 67% more unique external-call signatures than Etherscan’s Panoramix, and over 5x faster decompilation.
- [ExeBench: an ML-scale dataset of executable C functions](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3520312.3534867) (ACM SIGPLAN MAPS 2022, 2022) · [code](https://github.com/jordiae/exebench/tree/8b47d483c2e39ea40a304fbaac46e41a1a14272f) — ExeBench is a dataset, not a decompiler. It extracts C functions from GitHub and AnghaBench, makes them compilable by locating real headers and definitions or injecting synthetic ones, and generates input/output examples from function signatures with a C++ wrapper that runs each function. After exact deduplication the authors report 4,485,817 compilable and 687,843 executable functions, of which 124,612 have rich IO that a constant or identity implementation cannot pass; the paper also describes x86 and ARM assembly at O0, Os and Ofast for each row. Decompilation studies such as SLaDe and LLM4Decompile later used it to test whether decompiled functions re-execute correctly.
- [Fingerprinting and analysis of Bluetooth devices with automata learning](https://doi.org/10.1007/s10703-023-00425-y) (Formal Methods in System Design, 2022) · [code](https://github.com/apferscher/ble-learning) — Learns Mealy-machine models of Bluetooth Low Energy implementations on physical peripherals by active automata learning. An nRF52840 central running SweynTooth-derived firmware sends abstract BLE inputs, with error handling, repeated queries and caching for lost packets and non-deterministic replies. The authors learn connection-procedure models of six devices and legacy-pairing models of three, and use model differences to fingerprint their SoCs.
- [Fuzzware: Using Precise MMIO Modeling for Effective Firmware Fuzzing](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity22/presentation/scharnowski) (USENIX Security 2022, 2022) · [code](https://github.com/fuzzware-fuzzer/fuzzware) — Rehosts monolithic ARM Cortex-M firmware for fuzzing, using locally scoped symbolic execution to model memory-mapped I/O accesses and focus input mutations on values relevant to firmware logic.
- [How Machine Learning Is Solving the Binary Function Similarity Problem](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity22/presentation/marcelli) (USENIX Security 2022, 2022) · [code](https://github.com/Cisco-Talos/binary_function_similarity) — Measures binary function similarity research by re-implementing ten representative approaches from the security, programming-language and machine-learning communities in one IDA Pro-based framework, from fuzzy hashing (Catalog1, FunctionSimSearch) to graph neural network, NLP-encoder and transformer models. Two new datasets support six tasks that vary optimization, compiler, bitness and architecture, scored by AUC and ranking metrics, plus a firmware vulnerability-search case. The authors report that fuzzy hashing fails in harder cross-architecture settings, GNN-based models perform best in almost all tasks, and many recent models score similarly on common data.
- [jTrans: Jump-Aware Transformer for Binary Code Similarity Detection](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3533767.3534367) (ACM ISSTA 2022, 2022) · [code](https://github.com/vul337/jTrans) — jTrans embeds binary functions for similarity search with a BERT-style Transformer. Its jump-aware representation shares parameters between a direct jump’s operand token and the positional embedding of the jump target, so control flow enters the instruction sequence without a separate graph network. The model is pre-trained with masked language modeling and jump-target prediction, then fine-tuned with a cosine triplet objective on functions compiled from the same source at different optimization levels. The paper also introduces BinaryCorp, built by compiling C/C++ packages from Arch Linux’s official repositories and the AUR with gcc and g++.
- [Pop Quiz! Can a Large Language Model Help With Reverse Engineering?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.01142) (arXiv, 2022) — Tests whether OpenAI’s Codex model code-davinci-001 can answer reverse-engineering questions about C code in a zero-shot question-and-answer prompt. Programs come from templates the authors wrote, covering cybersecurity snippets and industrial-control algorithms such as PID controllers and filters, plus five single-file Linux malware samples from vx-underground. Each is presented as original or name-randomized source, or as Ghidra decompilation of gcc -O1 builds with debug information, without it, or stripped. After exploratory open-ended questions, generated true/false and short-answer questions about purpose, capabilities and key variables are graded against an answer oracle; Codex answered 72,754 of 136,260 questions correctly.
- [SymLM: Predicting Function Names in Stripped Binaries via Context-Sensitive Execution-Aware Code Embeddings](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3548606.3560612) (ACM CCS 2022, 2022) · [code](https://github.com/OSUSecLab/SymLM) — SymLM predicts function names for stripped binaries as sets of name words. It encodes each function’s instructions with a Trex model pretrained on micro-execution traces, fuses them with embeddings of callers, internal callees and external library calls taken from Ghidra’s interprocedural CFG, and scores words from a fixed vocabulary with an MLP decoder; name preprocessing and a word-embedding module, CodeWordNet, handle abbreviations and synonyms. On 1,431,169 functions from 16,027 binaries of 27 projects across x86, x64, ARM and MIPS, O0–O3 and four Hikari obfuscations, the authors report weighted average precision 0.634, recall 0.677 and F1 0.655, plus a firmware case study.
- [The Closer You Look, The More You Learn: A Grey-box Approach to Protocol State Machine Learning](https://doi.org/10.1145/3548606.3559365) (ACM CCS 2022, 2022) · [code](https://github.com/ChrisMcMStone/state-inspector) — StateInspector learns protocol Mealy machines by combining active message queries with snapshots of runtime memory, without requiring the target’s source code. It identifies candidate state-defining heap locations and uses taint and concolic analysis to merge states, evaluated by the authors on TLS 1.2 and WPA/2 implementations.
- [What Your Firmware Tells You Is Not How You Should Emulate It: A Specification-Guided Approach for Firmware Emulation](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3548606.3559386) (ACM CCS 2022, 2022) · [code](https://github.com/MCUSec/SEmu/tree/6dc8be91f6809f34af972a93c9dd972f85145a00) — SEmu builds microcontroller peripheral models from vendor reference manuals rather than from the firmware itself. An NLP pipeline built on the Stanford tagger and parser turns register descriptions, interrupt tables and DMA channel tables taken from the manuals into structured condition-action rules, which the emulator checks, executes and chains at run time to answer MMIO accesses and raise interrupts or DMA requests. A symbolic-execution diagnostic tool built on S2E and µEmu traces invalid states back to the rules likely responsible, so that a person can correct them. The authors also use the models for fuzzing and to check driver code against specification rules.
- [Asteria: Deep Learning-based AST-Encoding for Cross-platform Binary Code Similarity Detection](https://doi.org/10.1109/DSN48987.2021.00036) (2021 51st Annual IEEE/IFIP International Conference on Dependable Systems and Networks (DSN), 2021) — ASTERIA uses a Tree-LSTM to encode function ASTs and compare binary functions across platforms. It was evaluated on function pairs and used to search IoT firmware for vulnerabilities.
- [Automatic Firmware Emulation through Invalidity-guided Knowledge Inference](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity21/presentation/zhou) (USENIX Security 2021, 2021) · [code](https://github.com/MCUSec/uEmu/tree/c82fc0a36a28ab5f96001b0682adcf9783e20346) — µEmu emulates ARM Cortex-M microcontroller firmware without real hardware or hand-written peripheral models by symbolically executing the image with unknown peripheral registers as symbols. Invalidity-guided exploration detects invalid states such as unexpected infinite loops and steers execution toward valid paths, caching the chosen responses at individual peripheral access points in a tiered knowledge base whose rules range from simple storage to PC- and calling-context-based matching. During dynamic analysis the knowledge base answers peripheral reads while an integrated, modified AFL fuzzer supplies data-register input. The prototype extends S2E 2.0 with Cortex-M support.
- [Conware: Automated Modeling of Hardware Peripherals](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3433210.3437532) (ACM ASIA CCS 2021, 2021) · [code](https://github.com/ucsb-seclab/conware/tree/05dfdf5552d5c05dfca32137487fc8b3aadcd256) — Conware builds reusable software models of hardware peripherals from recordings of real firmware–peripheral interactions. An LLVM pass instruments Arduino source code to log MMIO reads, writes and interrupts on the real board, with existing hardware-in-the-loop recording as an alternative. Each recording becomes a per-peripheral graph whose read values are summarized as simple sub-models such as patterns; graphs are merged and optimized into automata that can be combined across peripherals and run in an Avatar²-based emulator. The authors model ten peripherals on an Arduino Due (SAM3X Cortex-M3) and emulate an uninstrumented smart-door-lock firmware from models trained only on vendor example code.
- [DICE: Automatic Emulation of DMA Input Channels for Dynamic Firmware Analysis](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/9519398) (IEEE S&P 2021, 2021) · [code](https://github.com/RiS3-Lab/DICE-DMA-Emulation/tree/2b3b8c8b722abdbd7ff9e35fc7b02914fddf75df) — DICE adds DMA input emulation to microcontroller firmware emulators that model neither peripherals nor DMA controllers. It watches firmware writes to the memory-mapped DMA controller region for pairs of source and destination transfer pointers, and infers the transfer direction from later firmware accesses. When firmware reads a peripheral-to-RAM destination buffer, DICE answers with analyzer-supplied input such as fuzzer data, growing buffer bounds conservatively. Prototypes patch the QEMU forks of P2IM (ARM Cortex-M) and a PIC32 emulator (MIPS M4K/M-Class). On 83 sample firmware covering 9 DMA controllers from 5 vendors, the authors report 33 of 37 input channels detected with no false positives, correct input for 21 of 22 buffers and 3.4% average overhead. Fuzzing 7 real firmware for 48 hours found 5 previously unknown bugs.
- [ECMO: Peripheral Transplantation to Rehost Embedded Linux Kernels](https://doi.org/10.1145/3460120.3484753) (Proceedings of the 2021 ACM SIGSAC Conference on Computer and Communications Security, 2021) — ECMO transplants peripheral drivers into embedded Linux kernels to boot firmware in QEMU. It evaluated 815 firmware images across 20 kernel versions, 37 device models, and 24 vendors; 710 reached a user-space shell.
- [From Library Portability to Para-rehosting: Natively Executing Microcontroller Software on Commodity Hardware](https://doi.org/10.14722/ndss.2021.24308) (Proceedings 2021 Network and Distributed System Security Symposium, 2021) — The paper uses a POSIX-based portable MCU and replaceable peripheral drivers to rehost MCU software for native execution and security testing on commodity hardware.
- [Jetset: Targeted Firmware Rehosting for Embedded Systems](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity21/presentation/johnson) (USENIX Security 2021, 2021) · [code](https://github.com/aerosec/jetset) — Jetset rehosts firmware only as far as an analyst-chosen goal address, such as the point where a fuzzer can inject input. Given the architecture, the MMIO address range, an entry point and the goal, it symbolically executes the firmware with angr against a modified QEMU, treating peripheral reads as symbolic, cycling interrupts, and steering toward the goal with CFG distances and Tabu search. Solved constraints become C peripheral models for QEMU that replay the inferred values and interrupts so the firmware boots concretely to the goal. The authors evaluate 13 targets across x86, ARM and Motorola ColdFire, including Linux on a Raspberry Pi 2, a BeagleBoard-xM bootloader, a SEL-751 relay, a Boeing 737 CMU-900 and nine P²IM firmware, and use the CMU model with AFL to find a privilege escalation in its VRTX kernel.
- [MappyLand: Fast, Accurate Mapping for Console Games](https://ojs.aaai.org/index.php/AIIDE/article/view/18892) (AIIDE 2021, 2021) · [code](https://github.com/faim-lab/mappy/tree/bbf99dd0f53c0986250c9020e3a7a75f9732b19f) — MappyLand, a Rust rewrite of the earlier Mappy system, instruments the FCEUMM NES emulator core to map action/adventure games as they are played from controller inputs. It hooks accesses to the picture-processing unit's scrolling registers and reads emulator memory to register scrolling, separate the playfield from menus, detect and track sprites, build grid-based tile maps and recognize room transitions. An online merging step combines observed rooms into a consistent room graph across backtracking and multiple play sessions. In a 34,514-frame test the authors report about 1.5 ms of mapping overhead per frame, against about 0.2 ms for emulation alone.
- [NetPlier: Probabilistic Network Protocol Reverse Engineering from Message Traces](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/netplier-probabilistic-network-protocol-reverse-engineering-from-message-traces/) (NDSS 2021, 2021) · [code](https://github.com/yapengye/NetPlier) — Uses multiple sequence alignment and probabilistic keyword-field inference to cluster network messages by type, evaluated across ten protocols with downstream format and state-machine analysis.
- [Noncompliance as Deviant Behavior: An Automated Black-box Noncompliance Checker for 4G LTE Cellular Devices](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3460120.3485388) (ACM CCS 2021, 2021) · [code](https://github.com/SyNSec-den/DIKEUE/tree/5975807883265e275ef533f18a0c21b56e3b4e8a) — DIKEUE checks 4G LTE control-plane (NAS and RRC) implementations in commercial phones and modems for noncompliance through differential testing rather than predefined security properties. Its FSM inference module runs TTT active automata learning in LearnLib through an LTE-specific adapter on a modified srsLTE network with a USRP B210 radio, adding a context checker, query cache, inconsistency resolver and reset without rebooting. An equivalence checker then encodes pairs of learned input-output finite state machines for the nuXmv model checker and enumerates diverse deviation-inducing input sequences, which the authors analyze manually.
- [Snipuzz: Black-box Fuzzing of IoT Firmware via Message Snippet Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2105.05445) (ACM CCS 2021, 2021) — Snipuzz fuzzes IoT firmware as a client, using device responses to infer message snippets that guide mutations. It was evaluated on 20 real-world IoT devices and compared with four IoT fuzzing approaches.
- [SoK: Enabling Security Analyses of Embedded Systems via Rehosting](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3433210.3453093) (ACM ASIA CCS 2021, 2021) · [code](https://github.com/rehosting/rehosting_sok) — Systematizes rehosting: moving embedded firmware from its physical hardware into a virtual environment that models enough hardware behavior for dynamic analysis such as fuzzing and symbolic execution. The authors measure why whole-system emulation does not scale using 2,574 hardware configurations, 1,956 device tree blobs from Linux 5.11.4 and 618 vendor ARM Cortex SVD files. They compare corpus CPU models with QEMU support, count unique peripherals, use driver source lines as a complexity proxy, and run a Monte Carlo simulation of adding peripheral models SoC by SoC. Supporting a random 10% of the SoCs would need about 3,947 peripherals for the Linux corpus and 1,730 for the ARM Cortex corpus, against 1,083 implemented in QEMU 5.2. The paper then classifies prior systems by how they emulate, replace, symbolically model, pass through to hardware or leave each layer unmodified, describes an iterative rehosting process with fidelity evaluation, and sets out open problems.
- [BaseSAFE: Baseband SAnitized Fuzzing through Emulation](https://doi.org/10.1145/3395351.3399360) (Proceedings of the 13th ACM Conference on Security and Privacy in Wireless and Mobile Networks, 2020) — BaseSAFE partially rehosts closed-source cellular baseband firmware for instrumented fuzzing and uses a sanitizing allocator to detect heap buffer overflows.
- [Binary Rewriting without Control Flow Recovery](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3385412.3385972) (PLDI 2020, 2020) · [code](https://github.com/GJDuck/e9patch) — E9Patch statically rewrites x86-64 binaries by inserting jumps to trampolines without moving any other instruction, so the original set of jump targets is preserved and control-flow recovery, symbolization and debug information are not needed. Where a five-byte jump does not fit, instruction punning, padding and successor or neighbor eviction overlap the jump with existing bytes or relocate neighboring instructions into their own trampolines; physical page grouping reduces the memory and file-size cost of scattered trampolines. Instruction locations come from a separate frontend, a basic linear disassembler in the evaluation. The authors evaluate SPEC2006, Ubuntu 16.04 system binaries, and Chrome and Firefox builds over 100 MB, and demonstrate binary repair and heap-write hardening with low fat pointers.
- [BinRec: Dynamic Binary Lifting and Recompilation](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3342195.3387550) (EuroSys 2020, 2020) · [code](https://github.com/trailofbits/binrec-tob) — BinRec lifts an executable to LLVM IR from dynamic traces instead of static disassembly, then lowers the IR back to a recovered binary, so compiler passes can optimize, harden or otherwise transform it. Tracing runs the binary under S²E, which translates the executed code through QEMU into LLVM IR; BinRec removes the emulation instrumentation, merges traces from several inputs into one program, and adds optimizations such as register de-aliasing and promotion of emulated state to locals. When the recovered binary reaches a path that was not traced, a configurable handler stops it, falls back to the original code, or lifts the new path incrementally. The authors report recovering all SPEC CINT2006 benchmarks, including C++ programs, and apply it to reoptimization, control-flow integrity, AddressSanitizer and SafeStack hardening, and partial deobfuscation of virtualization-obfuscated code.
- [Egalito: Layout-Agnostic Binary Recompilation](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3373376.3378470) (ASPLOS 2020, 2020) · [code](https://github.com/columbia/egalito) — Egalito statically recompiles stripped x86-64 and ARM64 binaries, using the metadata that position-independent executables and libraries carry to recover a complete disassembly in which every code and data cross-reference becomes an explicit link. Its layout-agnostic intermediate representation, EIR, stays close to machine code, so regenerated output is predictable, while a higher-level view supports analysis; data-flow analyses infer jump tables, which have no metadata. Because the original layout imposes no constraints, tools can move or rewrite code freely; the authors build nine, including continuous code randomization in which Egalito transforms itself, a retpoline defense, and software emulation of Intel CET. They report recovering the compiler's jump tables in 866 of 867 executables and libraries from 172 Debian packages, all tests passing in 90 of 149 transformed packages, and a 1.7% speedup on SPEC CPU 2006.
- [FirmAE: Towards Large-Scale Emulation of IoT Firmware for Dynamic Analysis](https://doi.org/10.1145/3427228.3427294) (ACSAC 2020, 2020) · [code](https://github.com/pr0v3rbs/FirmAE) — Extends Firmadyne with empirically derived interventions for boot, networking, NVRAM, kernel behavior, and other runtime failures, enabling Linux router and IP-camera firmware web services to run for dynamic analysis.
- [Frankenstein: Advanced Wireless Fuzzing to Exploit New Bluetooth Escalation Targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.09809) (USENIX Security 2020, 2020) — Frankenstein emulates Bluetooth firmware dumps and fuzzes a virtual modem. It evaluates Broadcom and Cypress stacks, finding three zero-click vulnerabilities, host escalation issues, and BlueFrag on Android.
- [HALucinator: Firmware Re-hosting Through Abstraction Layer Emulation](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity20/presentation/clements) (USENIX Security 2020, 2020) · [code](https://github.com/embedded-sec/halucinator) — HALucinator rehosts monolithic ARM Cortex-M firmware by identifying hardware-abstraction-library functions and replacing them with authored Python handlers and peripheral models. LibMatch locates library interfaces; the QEMU/Avatar2 implementation supports interactive I/O, while the paper-associated HAL-fuzz variant uses AFL-Unicorn for fuzzing.
- [How far we have come: testing decompilation correctness of C decompilers](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3395363.3397370) (ACM ISSTA 2020, 2020) · [code](https://github.com/monkbai/DecFuzzer) — Tests whether C decompilers preserve program behavior. The authors' DecFuzzer compiles a Csmith-generated program, decompiles the executable, substitutes the decompiled function for the original in the source, recompiles, and compares the two programs' outputs; equivalence-modulo-inputs (EMI) mutation then derives further test programs from the seeds. Across four popular decompilers the authors report 1,423 inputs that trigger decompilation errors and, after manual analysis, 13 bugs in two open-source decompilers. They argue that modern decompilers are making real progress on functional correctness, while type recovery and optimization still hinder accurate output.
- [Learning to Simulate Dynamic Environments with GameGAN](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/9156900) (CVPR 2020, 2020) · [code](https://github.com/nv-tlabs/GameGAN_code) — GameGAN learns an action-conditioned neural simulator of a game from recorded frames and the actions taken, without access to the game’s logic or engine. An action-conditioned LSTM dynamics engine with a stochastic latent variable predicts the next state, an optional external memory module builds an internal map so that revisited locations stay consistent, and a rendering engine decodes frames while separating static from dynamic components. Training is end-to-end with adversarial losses, including an action-conditioned discriminator, and a temporal cycle loss for memory consistency. The authors evaluate on a modified Pac-Man, a ghost-free Pac-Man-Maze and VizDoom “take cover”, training RL agents inside the learned simulators and testing them in the real games, and measure long-term consistency with a come-back-home task; a human-playable model of the official Pac-Man is shown qualitatively.
- [Machine Learning-Based Prototyping of Graphical User Interfaces for Mobile Apps](https://doi.org/10.1109/TSE.2018.2844788) (IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering, 2020) — ReDraw turns screenshots or design mock-ups into native Android GUI prototypes by detecting components, classifying widget types with a CNN, and assembling a plausible view hierarchy with a data-driven nearest-neighbor method.
- [Neural reverse engineering of stripped binaries using augmented control flow graphs](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3428293) (Proceedings of the ACM on Programming Languages (OOPSLA 2020), 2020) · [code](https://github.com/tech-srl/Nero/tree/7dac9f5748974ea8f39b5f29bb3c7d33f971077e) — Nero predicts names for procedures in stripped x86-64 Linux executables; it does not produce decompiled code. Static analysis reconstructs call sites, including imported API names, and augments their arguments with concrete and abstract values along control-flow paths. LSTM, Transformer and graph neural network encoders over the resulting augmented call-site graph feed a decoder that generates the name as subtokens. On a GNU-package test set the authors report 45.53 F1 for Nero-GNN, against 35.52 for an adapted DIRE and 33.66 for Debin.
- [On Manually Reverse Engineering Communication Protocols of Linux Based IoT Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.11981) (arXiv, 2020) — Presents a manual framework for reverse engineering communication protocols in embedded Linux IoT systems. It applies the framework to several systems and details a WeMo smart plug case study.
- [RetroWrite: Statically Instrumenting COTS Binaries for Fuzzing and Sanitization](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/9152762) (IEEE S&P 2020, 2020) · [code](https://github.com/HexHive/retrowrite) — RetroWrite statically rewrites 64-bit position-independent Linux binaries by generating reassembleable assembly, so instrumentation can be inserted as if at compile time. Instead of heuristics, it symbolizes references from the binary's own metadata: control-flow operands, PC-relative addressing that position-independent code must use, and data relocations become assembler labels, which separates references from scalars. On this base the authors build AFL coverage instrumentation and a binary-only AddressSanitizer. They report fuzzing throughput matching compiler instrumentation and 4.5x faster than AFL's QEMU mode, with more bugs found on LAVA-M, and binary ASan that runs 3x faster than Valgrind memcheck on SPEC CPU2006 and detects 80% more bugs in their evaluation, whose coverage tests use Juliet memory-corruption cases.
- [Towards usable automated detection of CPU architecture and endianness for arbitrary binary files and object code sequences](https://doi.org/10.1145/3374664.3375742) (Proceedings of the Tenth ACM Conference on Data and Application Security and Privacy, 2020) — The paper builds open datasets and tools to identify CPU architecture and endianness in binary files and object code. It evaluates classifiers on executable code sections, reporting over 98% accuracy.
- [Trex: Learning Execution Semantics from Micro-Traces for Binary Similarity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2012.08680) (arXiv, 2020) — Trex learns execution semantics from function micro-traces, then uses that knowledge to match semantically similar binary functions.
- [Automatic Wireless Protocol Reverse Engineering](https://www.usenix.org/conference/woot19/presentation/pohl) (13th USENIX Workshop on Offensive Technologies (WOOT 19), 2019) · [code](https://github.com/jopohl/urh) — Infers message formats of proprietary wireless protocols from demodulated physical-layer captures: it aligns messages by preamble and sync word, then uses RSSI and dedicated engines to label length, address, sequence-number and checksum fields, optionally using known addresses or field positions.
- [Coda: An End-to-End Neural Program Decompiler](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2019/hash/093b60fd0557804c8ba0cbf1453da22f-Abstract.html) (NeurIPS 2019, 2019) — Coda decompiles the assembly of short functions into C in two phases. An instruction-type-aware N-ary Tree-LSTM encoder and an attention-fed AST decoder first generate a code sketch; an ensemble of neural error predictors then proposes token corrections, each kept only if recompiling the candidate does not increase its edit distance to the input assembly. On synthetic benchmarks the authors report 82% average program accuracy after correction, against 12% for a sequence-to-sequence baseline with attention.
- [DIRE: A Neural Approach to Decompiled Identifier Naming](https://doi.org/10.1109/ASE.2019.00064) (ASE 2019, 2019) · [code](https://github.com/pcyin/dire) — Combines lexical and structural information from decompiler output to predict meaningful variable names, with a training and evaluation corpus derived from compiled C projects.
- [ICSREF: A Framework for Automated Reverse Engineering of Industrial Control Systems Binaries](https://doi.org/10.14722/ndss.2019.23271) (Proceedings 2019 Network and Distributed System Security Symposium, 2019) — ICSREF automates reverse engineering of PLC binaries compiled with CODESYS. It was evaluated on collected and in-house PLC binaries and used to generate an attack against a chemical process testbed.
- [InternalBlue - Bluetooth Binary Patching and Experimentation Framework](https://doi.org/10.1145/3307334.3326089) (Proceedings of the 17th Annual International Conference on Mobile Systems, Applications, and Services, 2019) — The paper reverse engineers Broadcom Bluetooth controllers and presents InternalBlue for patching their firmware. It evaluates the framework through vulnerability tests, bug fixes, and added security features.
- [LibreCAN: Automated CAN Message Translator](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3319535.3363190) (ACM CCS 2019, 2019) — LibreCAN reverse-engineers proprietary CAN message formats from traffic recorded at a vehicle's OBD-II port, without the OEM's DBC translation files. Phase 0 extracts candidate signal boundaries from bit-flip behaviour, extending the earlier READ algorithm; Phase 1 correlates extracted signals with standard OBD-II PIDs and smartphone IMU data to identify kinematic signals and estimate their scale and offset; Phase 2 filters the CAN IDs recorded while a driver performs 53 scripted body-related events to shortlist candidate IDs for each event.
- [Manticore: A User-Friendly Symbolic Execution Framework for Binaries and Smart Contracts](https://arxiv.org/abs/1907.03890) (arXiv, 2019) — Manticore uses dynamic symbolic execution to analyze binaries and Ethereum smart contracts. The paper presents its architecture and demonstrates bug finding and code verification used for commercial clients.
- [Towards Neural Decompilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/1905.08325) (arXiv, 2019) — The paper trains a neural machine translation decompiler using a compiler. It evaluates translation from LLVM IR and x86 assembly to C, successfully decompiling over 97% and 88% of its respective benchmarks.
- [A Cross-Architecture Instruction Embedding Model for Natural Language Processing-Inspired Binary Code Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/1812.09652) (arXiv, 2018) — The paper learns instruction embeddings that capture semantic relationships within and across architectures. It evaluates them on semantic basic-block comparison, outperforming a code-statistics approach.
- [Debin: Predicting Debug Information in Stripped Binaries](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3243734.3243866) (ACM CCS 2018, 2018) · [code](https://github.com/eth-sri/debin) — Debin predicts debug information for stripped ELF binaries on x86, x64 and ARM. After lifting code to BAP-IR, an Extremely randomized Trees classifier decides which registers and memory offsets hold source-level variables. A conditional random field, solved with Nice2Predict, then jointly predicts names and types for variables and functions, and the result is written back as DWARF sections. On 300 held-out binaries per architecture, the authors report 68.8% precision and 68.3% recall for x64 names and types.
- [From UI Design Image to GUI Skeleton: A Neural Machine Translator to Bootstrap Mobile GUI Implementation](https://doi.org/10.1145/3180155.3180240) (ICSE 2018, 2018) · [code](https://github.com/ccywch/UI2code) — UI2code translates a mobile UI image into an Android component hierarchy and layout skeleton using a neural encoder-decoder. The paper evaluates structural prediction and a small developer study of implementing layouts from generated skeletons.
- [NEMESYS: Network Message Syntax Reverse Engineering by Analysis of the Intrinsic Structure of Individual Messages](https://www.usenix.org/conference/woot18/presentation/kleber) (12th USENIX Workshop on Offensive Technologies (WOOT 18), 2018) · [code](https://github.com/vs-uulm/nemesys) — Infers approximate field boundaries within individual binary-protocol messages from changes in bit congruence, and introduces Format Match Score to compare inferred segmentation with known protocol dissectors.
- [pix2code: Generating Code from a Graphical User Interface Screenshot](https://doi.org/10.1145/3220134.3220135) (EICS 2018, 2018) · [code](https://github.com/tonybeltramelli/pix2code) — Learns screenshot-to-DSL generation for native Android XML, iOS Storyboards and web HTML/CSS interfaces, then compiles the DSL into platform UI code.
- [Game Engine Learning from Video](https://www.ijcai.org/proceedings/2017/518) (IJCAI 2017, 2017) — Learns a rule-based forward simulator of a 2D game from gameplay video. Using a supplied sprite palette, OpenCV locates sprites in each frame; matched sprites are converted into hand-designed fact types for animation, position, relative distance, x and y velocity and camera offset. Game Engine Search then scans the frames, and whenever the current engine’s predicted next frame differs from the real one by more than a threshold, it runs a greedy search over engines that add or modify condition–effect rules or mark them as player-controlled. Engines are ranked by pixel error and rule count. Trained on one Super Mario Bros. Level 1-1 video, the learned engine predicts frames of a different player’s video more accurately than a CNN frame predictor and a previous-frame baseline, and a value-iteration agent planning with it shows no significant difference from one using the Infinite Mario engine’s native forward simulation.
- [Mechanics Automatically Recognized via Interactive Observation: Jumping](https://doi.org/10.1145/3102071.3102104) (FDG 2017, 2017) — Infers platform-game jump mechanics by running controlled experiments in a modified FCEUX NES emulator. A person first plays each game to a safe state; the system then repeatedly holds the jump button for 1 to 120 frames, waits 120 frames and resets, recording every sprite from the PPU sprite table. Sprites that usually touch one another are merged into characters and tracked. A hand-designed four-state jump automaton (ground, rising with and without the button, falling) is then fitted with linear support vector regression, giving per-state velocity and gravity parameters plus minimum and maximum button-hold durations. Across 48 games (about 30% of NES platformers) and 52 characters, the authors cluster the learned jumps into three styles.
- [Ramblr: Making Reassembly Great Again](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss2017/ndss-2017-programme/ramblr-making-reassembly-great-again/) (NDSS 2017, 2017) — Ramblr produces reassembleable disassembly: assembly recovered from a stripped binary that can be edited and assembled again with an off-the-shelf assembler. Starting from angr's CFG recovery, it classifies the contents of data regions, then decides which immediates and data values are references to code or data that must become symbols. Base-pointer reattribution handles compiler-optimized references that point outside their target object, and sanity checks let Ramblr refuse to reassemble a binary it cannot handle safely, such as one that encrypts pointers. The authors evaluate 106 GNU Coreutils programs for x86 and x86-64 and 143 DARPA Cyber Grand Challenge binaries at six optimization levels. They report that Uroboros broke 22.64% of 32-bit Coreutils binaries at -O0 and 56.61% at -O1, while Ramblr broke none, and that Ramblr reassembled over 98% of CGC binaries at every level.
- [rev.ng: A Unified Binary Analysis Framework to Recover CFGs and Function Boundaries](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3033019.3033028) (CC 2017, 2017) · [code](https://github.com/revng/revng) — rev.ng lifts binaries to LLVM IR through QEMU's tiny code generator, so one front end covers every architecture QEMU supports and the output is compilable IR. On that IR the authors build architecture-independent analyses that recover a program's control-flow graph and function boundaries without symbols or debugging information. A value-range analysis (OSRA) bounds indirect jump targets such as switch tables, and further analyses handle predicated instructions, noreturn functions, tail calls and context-dependent control flow. On coreutils built for MIPS, ARM and x86-64 with GCC and clang at -O2, -O3 and -Os, its function-boundary accuracy is close to IDA Pro and better than BAP/ByteWeight and angr.
- [Rico: A Mobile App Dataset for Building Data-Driven Design Applications](https://doi.org/10.1145/3126594.3126651) (UIST 2017, 2017) — Rico is a dataset of Android app designs and a runtime mining system that needs no access to or modification of app source code. Crowd workers used free Google Play apps through a web interface to a device farm, recording interaction traces. An automated crawler then replayed each trace and kept exploring, using a screenshot-and-resource-id similarity heuristic to identify new UI states. The paper reports 10,811 traces and 72,219 unique UIs from 9,772 apps in 27 categories. Each UI has a screenshot, augmented view hierarchy, explored interactions, transition animations and a 64-dimensional autoencoder layout vector.
- [Statistical Deobfuscation of Android Applications](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/2976749.2978422) (ACM CCS 2016, 2016) — DeGuard reverses layout obfuscation in Android APKs, predicting original names for the fields, methods, classes and packages that ProGuard renames. Built on Soot, it represents an app as a dependency graph over program elements and keeps Android API and other unobfuscated names fixed. It then runs MAP inference with Nice2Predict on a conditional random field learned from 1,684 F-Droid apps, under constraints that keep the renamed APK semantically equivalent. On 100 held-out F-Droid apps obfuscated with ProGuard, names identical to the originals rise from 13.3% to 79.1%, and third-party library prediction reaches 91.3% precision and 91.0% recall.
- [Towards Automated Dynamic Analysis for Linux-based Embedded Firmware](https://doi.org/10.14722/ndss.2016.23415) (NDSS 2016, 2016) · [code](https://github.com/firmadyne/firmadyne) — Firmadyne crawls, extracts and emulates Linux-based firmware for network-connected commercial devices such as routers and IP cameras. It replaces the vendor kernel with pre-built, instrumented kernels for little-endian ARM and little- and big-endian MIPS, boots the extracted filesystem under QEMU full-system emulation with a userspace NVRAM library, infers the network configuration, then runs automated analyses including 60 known and 14 newly found exploits. From 23,035 images across 42 vendors, the authors extracted 9,486 and report 887 of the 1,971 network-reachable images vulnerable to at least one sampled exploit.
- [Automated Dynamic Firmware Analysis at Scale: A Case Study on Embedded Web Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/1511.03609) (arXiv, 2015) — The paper emulates firmware images and uses static and dynamic tools to find vulnerabilities in embedded web interfaces. It evaluated 1,925 images from 54 vendors and found vulnerabilities in 185 images.
